\implies
(iv)
is also clear, since for every
v\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
the corresponding constant function
\widetilde{v}:[t_{0},t_{1}]\to\mathbb{C}^{n},\ t\mapsto v
is contained in
\mathcal{V}_{t_{0},t_{1}}
, and therefore

\infty>{v}^{\mathrm{H}}\left(\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}M(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\right)v=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}{\widetilde{v}(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}M(t)\widetilde{v}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\geq 0.

It remains to show that
(iv)
\implies
(i)
.
For every
v\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
, let us define
f^{v}:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R},\ t\mapsto{v}^{\mathrm{H}}M(t)v
. We clearly have
f^{v}\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})
and

\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}f^{v}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t={v}^{\mathrm{H}}\left(\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}M(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\right)v\geq 0

for all
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathbb{T},\ t_{0}\leq t_{1}
, and thus
f^{v}(t)\geq 0
for a.e.
t\in\mathbb{T}
, because of
Lemma

A.8
.
In other words, we have
{v}^{\mathrm{H}}M(t)v\geq 0
for every
v\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
and for a.e.
t\in\mathbb{T}
, i.e.,
M\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
, as claimed.
∎

We obtain the following characterization of weakly differentiable solutions to
(KYP)
.

Corollary 4.3
.

A matrix function
Q\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))

is a solution of the
(KYP)
inequality (
4
) if and only if the bounded linear operator

\displaystyle\mathrm{KYP}(Q)_{t_{0},t_{1}}:L^{\infty}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{n})\times L^{2}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{m})\to L^{1}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{n})\times L^{2}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{m}),

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}x\\
u\end{bmatrix}\mapsto\begin{bmatrix}-{A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q-QA-\dot{Q}&{C}^{\mathrm{H}}-QB\\
C-{B}^{\mathrm{H}}Q&D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}x\\
u\end{bmatrix}

satisfies

\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}{\begin{bmatrix}x(t)\\
u(t)\end{bmatrix}}^{\mathrm{H}}\left(\mathrm{KYP}(Q)_{t_{0},t_{1}}\begin{bmatrix}x\\
u\end{bmatrix}\right)(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\geq 0

(29)

for all
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathbb{T},\ t_{0}\leq t_{1}
.

Proof.

The fact that the operator
\mathrm{KYP}(Q)_{t_{0},t_{1}}
is well-defined, linear and bounded is clear, since

\displaystyle\lVert(-{A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q-QA-\dot{Q})x+({C}^{\mathrm{H}}-QB)u\rVert_{L^{1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq(2\lVert A\rVert_{L^{1}}\lVert Q\rVert_{L^{\infty}}+\lVert\dot{Q}\rVert_{L^{1}})\lVert x\rVert_{L^{\infty}}+(\lVert C\rVert_{L^{2}}-\lVert Q\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert B\rVert_{L^{2}})\lVert u\rVert_{L^{2}},

\displaystyle\lVert(C-Q{B}^{\mathrm{H}})x+(D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}})u\rVert_{L^{2}}\leq(\lVert C\rVert_{L^{2}}+\lVert Q\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert B\rVert_{L^{2}})\lVert x\rVert_{L^{\infty}}+2\lVert D\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert u\rVert_{L^{2}}

for all
x\in L^{\infty}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{n})
and
u\in L^{2}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{m})
.
By defining

M_{Q}\coloneqq\begin{bmatrix}-{A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q-QA-\dot{Q}&{C}^{\mathrm{H}}-QB\\
C-{B}^{\mathrm{H}}Q&D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}}\end{bmatrix}\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n,n}),

we obtain that
Q
is a solution of the KYP inequality if and only if
M_{Q}(t)\geq 0
for a.e.
t\in\mathbb{T}
.
Furthermore, it holds that

\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}{\begin{bmatrix}x(t)\\
u(t)\end{bmatrix}}^{\mathrm{H}}\left(\mathrm{KYP}(Q)_{t_{0},t_{1}}\begin{bmatrix}x\\
u\end{bmatrix}\right)(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}{v(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}M_{Q}(t)v(t)\,\mathrm{d}t

for all
v=(x,u)\in\mathcal{V}_{t_{0},t_{1}}\coloneqq L^{\infty}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{n})\times L^{2}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{m})
.
The assertion then follows immediately from
Theorem

4.2
.
∎

Remark 4.4
.

Condition (
29
) can be interpreted as self-adjointness and positive semi-definiteness of the operator
\mathrm{KYP(Q)_{t_{0},t_{1}}}
.
In fact, denoting more in general

\langle f,g\rangle_{t_{0},t_{1}}=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}{g(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}f(t)\,\mathrm{d}t

whenever
f,g\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{k})
are such that
{g}^{\mathrm{H}}f\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C})
, one can easily see that

\left\langle\mathrm{KYP(Q)_{t_{0},t_{1}}}\begin{bmatrix}x\\
u\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\widetilde{x}\\
\widetilde{u}\end{bmatrix}\right\rangle=\left\langle\begin{bmatrix}x\\
u\end{bmatrix},\mathrm{KYP(Q)_{t_{0},t_{1}}}\begin{bmatrix}\widetilde{x}\\
\widetilde{u}\end{bmatrix}\right\rangle

for all
x,\widetilde{x}\in L^{\infty}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{n})
and
u,\widetilde{u}\in L^{2}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{m})
, so
\mathrm{KYP(Q)_{t_{0},t_{1}}}={\mathrm{KYP(Q)}}^{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathrm{t_{0},t_{1}}}
.
Similarly, (
29
) is equivalent to

\left\langle\mathrm{KYP(Q)_{t_{0},t_{1}}}\begin{bmatrix}x\\
u\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}x\\
u\end{bmatrix}\right\rangle\geq 0

for all
x\in L^{\infty}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{n})
and
u\in L^{2}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{m})
.
This property is also related to the extended concept of operators, introduced in
[
42
]
, which are symmetric positive semi-definite with respect to certain
spaces of port variables
.

4.1
Relation of the pH representation to the KYP inequality

In this section, we show that pointwise invertible solutions of (
4
) can be used to obtain a pH formulation of a given time-varying system (
1
).

Theorem 4.5
.

Suppose that
Q\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
is a solution of the
(KYP)
inequality (
4
) associated to the LTV system (
1
).
Then the system can be formulated as a
(pH)
system of the form (
18
) with Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}=V_{Q}
as in (
15
).

Proof.

Due to
Theorem

3.5
,
V_{Q}
is a storage function for (
1
).
Then, because of
Theorem

2.9
and
Theorem

2.19
, we may assume without loss of generality that

\displaystyle Q(t)

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}Q_{11}(t)&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle Q_{11}(t)\in\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{r(t)}_{++}(\mathbb{C}),

\displaystyle\text{for all }t\in\mathbb{T},

\displaystyle\dot{Q}(t)

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\dot{Q}_{11}(t)&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\dot{Q}_{11}(t)\in\mathbb{C}^{r(t),r(t)},

\displaystyle\text{for a.e.\penalty\ }t\in\mathbb{T},

where
r=\rank\circ\,Q:\mathbb{T}\to\{0,\ldots,n\}
is weakly decreasing.
Define the coefficients
K,J,R,G,P,S,N
following (
25
), noting that
A_{12}=0
and
C_{2}=0
, because of
Lemma

2.20
.
By construction, we have

\displaystyle Q(t)K(t)+{K(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}Q(t)

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{1}{2}\dot{Q}_{11}(t)&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}\frac{1}{2}\dot{Q}_{11}(t)&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\dot{Q}_{11}(t)&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}=\dot{Q}(t),

\displaystyle J(t)

\displaystyle=-{J}^{\mathrm{H}}(t),\ N=-{N}^{\mathrm{H}}(t),

and

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\begin{bmatrix}-Q(t)A(t)-{A(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}Q(t)-\dot{Q}(t)&{C(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}-Q(t)B(t)\\
C(t)-{B(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}Q(t)&D(t)+{D(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}-Q_{11}(t)A_{11}(t)-{A_{11}(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}-\dot{Q}_{11}(t)&0&{C_{1}(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}-Q_{11}(t)B_{1}(t)\\
0&0&0\\
C_{1}(t)-{B_{1}(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}Q_{11}(t)&0&D(t)+{D(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}2Q_{11}(t)R_{11}(t)Q_{11}(t)&0&2Q_{11}(t)P_{1}(t)\\
0&0&0\\
2{P_{1}(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}Q_{11}(t)&0&2S(t)\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=2\begin{bmatrix}Q_{11}(t)&0&0\\
0&I_{n-r(t)}&0\\
0&0&I_{m}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}R_{11}(t)&0&P_{1}(t)\\
0&0&0\\
{P_{1}(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}&0&S(t)\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}Q_{11}(t)&0&0\\
0&I_{n-r(t)}&0\\
0&0&I_{m}\end{bmatrix}=2{V(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}W(t)V(t),

where
V(t)\coloneqq\operatorname{diag}(Q_{11}(t),I_{n-r(t)},I_{m})\in\operatorname{GL}_{n+m}(\mathbb{C})
, and therefore

W(t)={V(t)}^{-\mathrm{H}}\left({V(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}W(t)V(t)\right)V(t)^{-1}\geq 0,

for a.e.
t\in\mathbb{T}
. Thus, the conditions for
(pH)
are satisfied, and we have obtained a pH representation of (
1
) with Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}=V_{Q}
.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

Assuming complete reconstructability, it was shown in
[
2
, Lemma 3]
that the solutions
Q
to
(KYP)
are pointwise invertible. However, in contrast to the LTI case, the pH representation of LTV systems can be obtained without further invertibility assumptions on
Q
. The reason for this is that the additional term
K
in
(pH)
allows for more freedom in choosing
Q
that can also be non-invertible.

4.2
Further remarks on the solvability of KYP inequalities

In order to study the solvability of the KYP inequality, one has to solve the linear matrix inequality (
4
). In the LTI case, this is usually done using a quadratic programming approach
[
7
]
or by computing the invariant subspaces of matrix pencils
[
4
]
. For the LTV case, there have been attempts to solve this differential matrix inequality directly
[
2
]
. However, most of these methods are numerically infeasible, especially for large-scale systems.
In the context of dissipativity, solutions
Q
to (
4
) are also required to be pointwise positive semidefinite or even positive definite. Furthermore, in a LTV setting,
Q
is often required to have a constant rank.

An approach to compute such solutions is available if
(D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}})>0
pointwise. Then using the Schur complement, one can solve the
(KYP)
in (
4
) via the solution of a differential Riccati inequality of the form

-{A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q-QA-\dot{Q}-(QB-{C}^{\mathrm{H}})(D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}})^{-1}({B}^{\mathrm{H}}Q-C)\geq 0,

and, as a special case, one can solve the equality case, see
[
1
,
20
,
47
]
for a detailed analysis of these equations, and
[
6
,
17
,
18
,
30
]
for numerical methods.

If
(D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}})\geq 0
is pointwise but singular, then a strategy to solve the
(KYP)
can be devised only on the non-singular part of
(D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}})
as presented in
[
2
]
or for the time-invariant case in
[
4
]
. Under constant rank assumptions, a recursive procedure is proposed to reduce the order of the state space system until a nonsingular
(D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}})
is encountered. Then, one can apply the Schur complement and solve the Riccati inequality on the reduced system.

Alternatively, instead of solving the
(KYP)
, one can use the equality case for the quadratic form associated with (
4
) by multiplying from the left by
{z}^{\mathrm{H}}=\begin{bmatrix}{x}^{\mathrm{H}}&{u}^{\mathrm{H}}\end{bmatrix}
and from the right by
z
. This results in an associated differential-algebraic system, similar to the approach to solving the
(KYP)
in the LTI case, see
[
4
]
. In the LTV case, the associated differential-algebraic system is given by

\begin{bmatrix}\phantom{-}0&I&0\\
-I&0&0\\
\phantom{-}0&0&0\end{bmatrix}\frac{d}{dt}\begin{bmatrix}\lambda\\
x\\
u\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}0&A&B\\
{A}^{\mathrm{H}}&0&-{C}^{\mathrm{H}}\\
{B}^{\mathrm{H}}&-C&D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\lambda\\
x\\
u\end{bmatrix}.

Inserting the ansatz
\lambda=Qx
, we get the two equations
0=-(\dot{Q}+QA+{A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q)x+({C}^{\mathrm{H}}-QB)u
and
0=(C-{B}^{\mathrm{H}}Q)x+(D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}})u
, which correspond to the two equations obtained by multiplying with
z
from the right in the equality case of (
4
).

Remark 4.7
.

Equivalently to the
(KYP)
inequality (
4
), one can solve a
Lur’e type inequality

\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{L}(t)&\mathcal{W}(t)\\
{\mathcal{W}}^{\mathrm{H}}(t)&(D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}})(t)\end{bmatrix}\geq 0,

where

\displaystyle{A}^{\mathrm{H}}(t)Q(t)+Q(t)A(t)+\dot{Q}(t)

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{L}(t)

\displaystyle{C}^{\mathrm{H}}(t)-P(t)B(t)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{W}(t)

for
Q(t)={Q}^{\mathrm{H}}(t)\geq 0
pointwise. Most of the literature on the subject, see e.g.
[
9
,
22
,
35
,
48
]
, considers the solution of the
Lur’e equation

\displaystyle Q(t)A(t)+{A}^{\mathrm{H}}(t)Q(t)+\dot{Q}(t)=-L(t){L}^{\mathrm{H}}(t),

\displaystyle C(t)-P(t)B(t)={L}^{\mathrm{H}}(t)W(t),

\displaystyle D(t)+{D}^{\mathrm{H}}(t)={W}^{\mathrm{H}}(t)W(t).

This is again a special case that restricts the solution space of the
(KYP)
. Thus, we refrain from looking at this approach in more detail.

5
Passivity and its implications for pH systems

We have already seen in Section
3
that port-Hamiltonian systems are passive, that is,
(pH)
implies
(Pa)
. In this section, we derive the extra conditions under which the converse implication holds. For this analysis, we proceed via the available storage as discussed in Section
2.2
.

5.1
Available storage

We define the available storage for LTV systems, as it was introduced, e.g. in
[
26
,
35
]
.

Definition 5.1
.

The
available storage
of the LTV system (
1
) at time
t_{0}\in\mathbb{T}
and state
x_{0}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
is

V_{a}(t_{0},x_{0})=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}(t_{1},u)\,:\,t_{1}\geq t_{0},\\
x(t_{0})=x_{0}\end{subarray}{}}\left(-\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\realPart({y(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}u(t))\,\mathrm{d}t\right)=-\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}(t_{1},u)\,:\,t_{1}\geq t_{0},\\
x(t_{0})=x_{0}\end{subarray}{}}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\realPart({y(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}u(t))\,\mathrm{d}t,

(30)

where the supremum (or infimum) is taken among all times
t_{1}\in\mathbb{T},\ t_{1}\geq t_{0}
and inputs
u\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n})
, and
x\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n})
and
y\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m})
denote the state and output trajectories uniquely determined by
u
and by the condition
x(t_{0})=x_{0}
.

Since the supremum in
Definition

5.1
is taken over a nonempty set, it is always well defined.
Furthermore, since for
t_{1}=t_{0}
the integral in (
30
) vanishes, the available storage is always nonnegative.
We can then intepret it as a possibly infinite-valued function
V_{a}:\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}\to[0,+\infty]
.
If we actually have
V_{a}(t_{0},x_{0})<+\infty
for all
t_{0}\in\mathbb{T}
and
x_{0}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
, we say that the available storage is finite.

The finiteness of the available storage is closely related to the passivity of the system
[
26
,
35
]
. In fact, in
[
43
]
the following result is proven.

Theorem 5.2
.

Consider an LTV system of the form (
1
). Then the following statements are equivalent.

(i)

The system is passive.

(ii)

There exists
\beta:\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
such that for every
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathbb{T},\ t_{0}\leq t_{1}
and state-input-output solution
(x,u,y)
it holds that

\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\realPart\big({y}^{\mathrm{H}}(t)u(t)\big)\,\mathrm{d}t\geq\beta\big(t_{0},x(t_{0})\big).

(iii)

The available storage function is finite.

(iv)

The available storage function is of the form
V_{a}=V_{Q}
for some matrix function
Q:\mathbb{T}\to\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C})
.

Since the available storage of a passive LTV system is the minimal storage function
[
26
]
, it is then natural to summarize its properties in the following corollary from
[
43
]
.

Corollary 5.3
.

The available storage of a passive LTV system of the form (
1
) is of the form
V_{a}=V_{Q}
for some
Q\in\AUC_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
and is minimal among all storage functions of (
1
), in the sense that
V_{a}(t_{0},x_{0})\leq V(t_{0},x_{0})
holds for every storage function
V:\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
,
t_{0}\in\mathbb{T}
and
x_{0}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
.

A trivial but very relevant consequence, which justifies our focus on quadratic storage functions, is then the following observation.

Corollary 5.4
.

Every passive
(Pa)
LTV system of the form (
1
) has a quadratic storage function
V_{Q}
for some
Q\in\AUC_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
.

Corollary

5.4
has important ramifications. Since every passive
(Pa)
LTV system has a quadratic storage function
V_{Q}
with
Q\in\AUC_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
, the properties studied in
Section

2.2
, most notably the null space decomposition, can be applied.
Furthermore, one can relate the matrix function
Q
inducing a quadratic storage function to the
(KYP)
inequality and to
(pH)
formulations, while this cannot be done for general storage functions.

5.2
Relation of passivity and KYP inequalities

It is well known that the (algebraic) KYP inequality associated with a passive LTI system always admits a solution
Q\in\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C})
, see, e.g.
[
13
, Proposition 1]
and
[
35
, Corollary 3.4]
. In this subsection, we investigate what can be said in the case of LTV systems, distinguishing between the different regularity properties of
Q
.

If
Q\in\BV_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
then we characterize the quadratic storage functions in terms of an
integral KYP inequality
as follows.

Theorem 5.5
.

Let
Q\in\BV_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
.
Then
V_{Q}
, as defined in (
15
), is a storage function for (
1
) if and only if
Q
satisfies the
integral KYP inequality

\begin{bmatrix}-\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\big({A(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}Q(t)+Q(t)A(t)\big)\,\mathrm{d}t+Q(t_{0})-Q(t_{1})&\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\big({C(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}-Q(t)B(t)\big)\,\mathrm{d}t\\
\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\big(C(t)-{B(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}Q(t)\big)\,\mathrm{d}t&\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\big(D(t)+{D(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}\big)\,\mathrm{d}t\end{bmatrix}\geq 0

(31)

for every
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathbb{T},\ t_{0}\leq t_{1}
.

Proof.

Starting from (
1
) expressed as

\begin{bmatrix}-\dot{x}\\
y\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}-A&-B\\
C&D\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}x\\
u\end{bmatrix},

we obtain

-{x}^{\mathrm{H}}Q\dot{x}+{u}^{\mathrm{H}}y={\begin{bmatrix}Qx\\
u\end{bmatrix}}^{\mathrm{H}}\begin{bmatrix}-\dot{x}\\
y\end{bmatrix}={\begin{bmatrix}x\\
u\end{bmatrix}}^{\mathrm{H}}\begin{bmatrix}-QA&-QB\\
C&D\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}x\\
u\end{bmatrix},

and therefore

-\frac{1}{2}({\dot{x}}^{\mathrm{H}}Qx+{x}^{\mathrm{H}}Q\dot{x})+\realPart({y}^{\mathrm{H}}u)=\frac{1}{2}{\begin{bmatrix}x\\
u\end{bmatrix}}^{\mathrm{H}}\begin{bmatrix}-QA-{A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q&{C}^{\mathrm{H}}-QB\\
C-{B}^{\mathrm{H}}Q&D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}x\\
u\end{bmatrix},

by extracting the real part.
Furthermore, for every state-input-output solution
(x,u,y)
of (
1
), exploiting the properties of the Riemann-Stieltjes integral (see Section
D
in the appendix), we have

\displaystyle V_{Q}\big(t_{0},x(t_{0})\big)-V_{Q}\big(t_{1},x(t_{1})\big)+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\realPart\big({y(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}u(t)\big)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{2}\big({x(t_{0})}^{\mathrm{H}}Q(t_{0})x(t_{0})-{x(t_{1})}^{\mathrm{H}}Q(t_{1})x(t_{1})\big)+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\realPart\big({y(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}u(t)\big)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\qquad=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\Big(\,\mathrm{d}\big({x}^{\mathrm{H}}Q(t)x\big)\Big)+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\realPart\big({y(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}u(t)\big)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\left(-\frac{1}{2}{x}^{\mathrm{H}}(\,\mathrm{d}Q)x\right)+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\left(-\frac{1}{2}({\dot{x}}^{\mathrm{H}}Qx+{x}^{\mathrm{H}}Q\dot{x})+\realPart({y}^{\mathrm{H}}u)\right)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{2}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}{\begin{bmatrix}x\\
u\end{bmatrix}}^{\mathrm{H}}\begin{bmatrix}-\,\mathrm{d}Q-(QA+{A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q)\,\mathrm{d}t&({C}^{\mathrm{H}}-QB)\,\mathrm{d}t\\
(C-{B}^{\mathrm{H}}Q)\,\mathrm{d}t&(D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}})\,\mathrm{d}t\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}x\\
u\end{bmatrix},

for every
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathbb{T},\ t_{0}\leq t_{1}
.
In particular,
V_{Q}
is a storage function if and only if the Riemann-Stieltjes integral inequality

\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}{\begin{bmatrix}x\\
u\end{bmatrix}}^{\mathrm{H}}\begin{bmatrix}-\,\mathrm{d}Q-(QA+{A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q)\,\mathrm{d}t&({C}^{\mathrm{H}}-QB)\,\mathrm{d}t\\
(C-{B}^{\mathrm{H}}Q)\,\mathrm{d}t&(D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}})\,\mathrm{d}t\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}x\\
u\end{bmatrix}\geq 0

(32)

holds for every state-input solution
(x,u)
of (
1
) and
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathbb{T},\ t_{0}\leq t_{1}
.
It is then sufficient to show that (
32
) is equivalent to (
31
).
In fact, if (
31
) holds, since
Q(t_{0})-Q(t_{1})=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\,\mathrm{d}Q
, it follows immediately from
Theorem

D.16
that (
32
) holds for all
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathbb{T},\ t_{0}\leq t_{1}
.

For the converse implication suppose now that (
32
) holds for all state-input solutions
(x,u)
of (
1
) and
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathbb{T},\ t_{0}\leq t_{1}
.
In particular, this holds for all constant input functions
u(t)\equiv u\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
.
For constant inputs, we have that

x(t)=X(t)x(t_{0})+\left(X(t)\int_{t_{0}}^{t}X(s)^{-1}B(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\right)u=X(t)x_{0}+Y(t)u

for all
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
, where
X\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
is any fundamental solution of (
1
) and

Y:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{C}^{n,m},\qquad t\mapsto X(t)\int_{t_{0}}^{t}X(s)^{-1}B(s)\,\mathrm{d}s.

Thus,

\begin{bmatrix}x(t)\\
u(t)\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}X(t)&Y(t)\\
0&I_{m}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}x(t)\\
u\end{bmatrix}

for all
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
.
In particular, we can rewrite (
32
) as

{\begin{bmatrix}x_{0}\\
u\end{bmatrix}}^{\mathrm{H}}\left(\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}{\begin{bmatrix}X&Y\\
0&I_{m}\end{bmatrix}}^{\mathrm{H}}\begin{bmatrix}-\,\mathrm{d}Q-(QA+{A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q)\,\mathrm{d}t&({C}^{\mathrm{H}}-QB)\,\mathrm{d}t\\
(C-{B}^{\mathrm{H}}Q)\,\mathrm{d}t&(D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}})\,\mathrm{d}t\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}X&Y\\
0&I_{m}\end{bmatrix}\right)\begin{bmatrix}x_{0}\\
u\end{bmatrix}\geq 0

for all
x_{0}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
and
u\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
, and therefore

\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}{\begin{bmatrix}X&Y\\
0&I_{m}\end{bmatrix}}^{\mathrm{H}}\begin{bmatrix}-\,\mathrm{d}Q-(QA+{A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q)\,\mathrm{d}t&({C}^{\mathrm{H}}-QB)\,\mathrm{d}t\\
(C-{B}^{\mathrm{H}}Q)\,\mathrm{d}t&(D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}})\,\mathrm{d}t\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}X&Y\\
0&I_{m}\end{bmatrix}\geq 0.

Note that
Y\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n,m})
, since

\lVert Y(t)\rVert\leq\lVert X\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert X^{-1}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\lVert B(s)\rVert\,\mathrm{d}s\leq(t_{1}-t_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}\lVert X\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert X^{-1}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert B\rVert_{L^{2}}.

In particular,

\begin{bmatrix}X&Y\\
0&I_{m}\end{bmatrix}^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}X^{-1}&-X^{-1}Y\\
0&I_{m}\end{bmatrix}\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n+m,n+m})

also holds.
We can then apply
Theorem

D.16
to conclude that (
31
) holds.
∎

Note that
Theorem

5.5
only requires
Q
to be of bounded variation, although we already know from
Theorem

2.18
that if
V_{Q}
is a storage function, then the stronger condition
Q\in\AUC_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
holds.
Furthermore, since for
Q
of bounded variation the classical derivative
\dot{Q}
is defined a.e. on
\mathbb{T}
, one might wonder whether it is really necessary to change from the differential inequality
(KYP)
(
4
) to the integral KYP inequality (
31
).
The following result sheds light on the apparent gap.

Corollary 5.6
.

Let
Q\in\BV_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
. If
V_{Q}
is a storage function for (
1
), then
Q
is a solution of the differential KYP inequality (
4
).
Conversely, if
Q\in\AUC_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
and
Q
is a solution of the differential KYP inequality (
4
), then
V_{Q}
is a storage function.

Proof.

Suppose first that
V_{Q}
is a storage function, in particular
Q
is a solution of the integral KYP (
31
) as in
Theorem

5.5
.
Since
Q\in\BV_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
, its time derivative
\dot{Q}(t)
exists for a.e.
t\in\mathbb{T}
in the classical sense, i.e.

\mathbb{T}_{0}=\left\{t\in\mathbb{T}\;\;\middle|\;\;\dot{Q}(t)=\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{Q(t+h)-Q(t)}{h}\text{ exists and is finite}\right\}

satisfies
\lvert\mathbb{T}\setminus\mathbb{T}_{0}\rvert=0
.
From the integral KYP inequality we deduce that

\begin{bmatrix}\displaystyle\frac{Q(t)-Q(t+h)}{h}-\frac{1}{h}\int_{t}^{t+h}\big({A(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}Q(t)+Q(t)A(t)\big)\,\mathrm{d}t&\displaystyle\frac{1}{h}\int_{t}^{t+h}\big({C(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}-Q(t)B(t)\big)\,\mathrm{d}t\\
\displaystyle\frac{1}{h}\int_{t}^{t+h}\big(C(t)-{B(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}Q(t)\big)\,\mathrm{d}t&\displaystyle\frac{1}{h}\int_{t}^{t+h}\big(D(t)+{D(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}\big)\,\mathrm{d}t\end{bmatrix}\geq 0

holds for all
t\in\mathbb{T}_{0}
and
h>0
such that
t+h\in\mathbb{T}
.
Going to the limit for
h\to 0
we then obtain

\begin{bmatrix}-\dot{Q}(t)-{A(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}Q(t)-Q(t)A(t)&{C(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}-Q(t)B(t)\\
C(t)-{B(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}Q(t)&D(t)+{D(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}\end{bmatrix}\geq 0,

i.e.,
Q
is a solution of the differential KYP inequality.

Suppose now that
Q\in\AUC_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
is a solution of the differential KYP inequality.
In particular, for every
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathbb{T},\ t_{0}\leq t_{1}
we have

Q(t_{1})-Q(t_{0})\leq\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\dot{Q}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\implies Q(t_{0})-Q(t_{1})\geq-\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\dot{Q}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t.

We deduce that

\begin{bmatrix}Q(t_{0})-Q(t_{1})-\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}({A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q+QA)\,\mathrm{d}t&\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}({C}^{\mathrm{H}}-QB)\,\mathrm{d}t\\
\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}(C-{B}^{\mathrm{H}}Q)\,\mathrm{d}t&\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}(D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}})\end{bmatrix}\geq\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\begin{bmatrix}-\dot{Q}-{A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q-QA&{C}^{\mathrm{H}}-QB\\
C-{B}^{\mathrm{H}}Q&D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}}\end{bmatrix}\,\mathrm{d}t\geq 0,

and therefore
Q
is a solution of the integral KYP inequality.
By
Theorem

5.5
then
V_{Q}
is a storage function.
∎

Remark 5.7
.

An important consequence of
Corollary

5.6
is that, for solutions
Q\in\AUC_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
, the differential KYP inequality (
4
) and the integral KYP inequality (
31
) are equivalent.
However, for more general solutions
Q\in\BV_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
, the integral KYP inequality is stricter than the differential KYP inequality.

Consider, for example, the stationary system
\dot{x}=0
, where
A,B,C,D
are identically zero, and any
Q\in\BV_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))\setminus\AUC_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
, e.g. the Cantor function.
On the one hand,
\dot{Q}=0
a.e. in
\mathbb{T}
, thus the differential KYP inequality is trivially satisfied.
On the other hand,
V_{Q}
cannot be a storage function, since
Q\notin\AUC_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
, thus it cannot be a solution of the integral KYP inequality.

Remark 5.8
.

Consider an LTV system of the form

\dot{x}(t)=u(t),\qquad y(t)=C(t)x(t)

(33)

for some
C\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n,n})
, where the state, input and output variables have the same dimension
n
.
Since
A=0
,
B=I_{n}
and
D=0
, the differential KYP inequality (
4
) can be written as

\begin{bmatrix}-\dot{Q}&{C}^{\mathrm{H}}-Q\\
C-Q&0\end{bmatrix}\geq 0.

Equivalently, the differential KYP inequality (
4
) admits a solution
Q\in\AUC_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
if and only if
C\in\AUC_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
with
\dot{C}\leq 0
(i.e., if
C
is pointwise Hermitian positive semi-definite and weakly decreasing, due to
Lemma

2.17
). In that case, the solution is uniquely determined as
Q=C
.
In particular, for every weakly decreasing
C:\mathbb{T}\to\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C})
, the matrix function
C
itself is a solution of the differential KYP inequality (
4
), thus the LTV system (
33
) is passive
(Pa)
with storage function
V_{C}
due to
Corollary

5.6
, and therefore it also has nonnegative supply
(NN)
, because of
Theorem

3.5
.
If additionally
C\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
, then
C
is an absolutely continuous solution of the
(KYP)
inequality. Thus, by
Theorem

4.5
(
33
) also admits a
(pH)
representation.

Remark

5.8
is particularly useful to construct examples as follows.

Example 5.9
.

Consider the LTV system

\dot{x}(t)=u(t),\qquad y(t)=\begin{cases}x(t)&\text{for }t<0,\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

(34)

in the time interval
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{R}
.
In particular,
y(t)=C(t)x(t)
with

C(t)=\begin{cases}1&\text{for }t<0,\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Clearly
C:\mathbb{T}\to\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{1}_{+}(\mathbb{C})
is weakly decreasing and, therefore, (
34
) is passive with storage function
V_{C}
, as discussed in
Remark

5.8
.
In particular,
Q=C\in\AUC_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{1}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
is a solution of the differential KYP inequality (
4
), although it is discontinuous.

Remark 5.10
.

One could consider requesting the weaker condition
Q\in\AUC_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
instead of
Q\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
when defining
(pH)
systems, that is, in
Definition

1.1
.
In fact, in that case we obtain, analogously as in the proof of
Theorem

3.5
, that

\begin{bmatrix}-\dot{Q}-{A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q-QA&{C}^{\mathrm{H}}-QB\\
C-{B}^{\mathrm{H}}Q&D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}Q&0\\
0&I_{m}\end{bmatrix}W\begin{bmatrix}Q&0\\
0&I_{m}\end{bmatrix}

holds a.e. on
\mathbb{T}
, i.e.,
Q
is still a solution of the differential KYP inequality (
4
).
Then, due to
Corollary

5.6
, we deduce that
V_{Q}
is a storage function and (
1
) is passive
(Pa)
. However, we do not have an immediate way to recover a power balance equation or associate a Dirac structure for the system.
In fact, the system may be dissipative even when
W=0
, as the following example shows.

Example 5.11
.

Consider the LTV system

\dot{x}(t)=u(t),\qquad y(t)=\breve{\mathfrak{c}}(t)x(t),

in the time interval
\mathbb{T}=(0,1)
, where
\breve{\mathfrak{c}}\in\AUC(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{1}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))\setminus W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{1}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
is the
reverse Cantor function

\breve{\mathfrak{c}}:(0,1)\to[0,1],\ t\mapsto\mathfrak{c}(1-t)
, which coincides with its singular part but is weakly monotonically decreasing, in particular
\breve{\mathfrak{c}}\in\AUC((0,1))\setminus W^{1,1}((0,1),\mathbb{R})
.

Following the observations in
Remark

5.10
, we rewrite the system in the form (
2
) with
J=R=K=S=N=P=0
,
G=1
, and
Q=\breve{\mathfrak{c}}
. This system satisfies all required properties, since
\dot{Q}=0
a.e. on
(0,1)
, and thus
Q\in\AUC_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{1}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
is a solution of the differential KYP inequality (
4
) and the system is passive.
Furthermore, for the input signal
u\equiv 0
the system is stationary, and therefore the dissipation inequality

V_{Q}\big(t_{1},x(t_{1})\big)-V_{Q}\big(t_{0},x(t_{0})\big)=\frac{1}{2}\big(\breve{\mathfrak{c}}(t_{1})-\breve{\mathfrak{c}}(t_{0})\big)x(t_{0})^{2}<0

holds strictly for all
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathbb{T}
such that
\breve{\mathfrak{c}}(t_{0})>\breve{\mathfrak{c}}(t_{1})
.
Thus, the dissipation of this system does not originate from the dissipation operator
W=0
, but from the non-zero singular part of
Q
.

Note that in
Definition

1.2
, we required the solutions of inequality
(KYP)
to be in
W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
. This condition is relevant for the transition from
(KYP)
to
(pH)
, as discussed in
Remark

5.10
. While
Corollary

5.6
shows that the matrix function
Q
inducing a quadratic storage function
V_{Q}
is always a solution of (
4
), in general we can only guarantee
Q\in\AUC_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
, see, e.g.,
Example

5.11
.
However, since
W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))\subseteq\AUC_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
, we have the following result.

Theorem 5.12
.

Suppose that
V_{Q}
with
Q\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
is a storage function for a passive
(Pa)
LTV system. Then
Q
is a solution of the
(KYP)
inequality (
4
).
In particular, the LTV system admits a
(pH)
representation.

In view of
Theorem

5.12
it is interesting to derive sufficient conditions for a passive LTV system to have a storage function
V_{Q}
with
Q\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
. In
[
2
]
it is mentioned that, for
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{R}
and under the strong regularity assumptions
A\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}^{n,n})
,
B\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}^{n,m})
,
C\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}^{m,n})
and
D\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}^{m,m})
, if the system is completely reachable, satisfies condition
D(t)+{D(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}>0
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
, and has nonnegative supply
(NN)
, then the Riccati differential equation

{A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q+QA+\dot{Q}+({C}^{\mathrm{H}}-QB)(D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}})^{-1}(C-{B}^{\mathrm{H}}Q)=0

(35)

has a solution
Q\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
with
\lim_{t\to\infty}Q(t)=0
, solving the minimization problem

-{x(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}Q(t)x(t)=2\inf_{u\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}^{m})}\int_{t}^{\infty}\realPart\big({y(s)}^{\mathrm{H}}u(s)\big)\,\mathrm{d}s.

From this it follows that
Q\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
induces the available storage function, i.e.
V_{a}=V_{Q}
. In the following,
using a different approach, we prove that a similar result holds in our more general setting.

The following lemma allows us to combine two storage functions defined on smaller time subintervals to obtain one defined on a larger time interval.

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
\mathbb{T}_{1},\mathbb{T}_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{T}
be two time subintervals such that
\inf(\mathbb{T}_{1})\leq\inf(\mathbb{T}_{2})
and
\sup(\mathbb{T}_{1})\leq\sup(\mathbb{T}_{2})
, and let
V_{1}:\mathbb{T}_{1}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
and
V_{2}:\mathbb{T}_{2}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
be storage functions for an LTV system (
1
), restricted to the corresponding time subintervals. Suppose that there exists
t_{0}\in\mathbb{T}_{1}\cap\mathbb{T}_{2}
such that
V_{1}(t_{0},x)=V_{2}(t_{0},x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Let
\mathbb{T}_{0}\coloneqq\mathbb{T}_{1}\cup\mathbb{T}_{2}
and let

V:\mathbb{T}_{0}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\mathbb{R},\qquad t\mapsto\begin{cases}V_{1}(t)&\text{for }t<t_{0},\\
V_{2}(t)&\text{for }t\geq t_{0}.\end{cases}

Then
V
is a storage function for (
1
) restricted to
\mathbb{T}_{0}
.

Proof.

It is clear from the assumption that
V(t,x)\geq 0
and
V(t,0)=0
hold for all
t\in\mathbb{T}_{0}
and
x(t)\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
Suppose now that
(x,u,y)
is a state-input-output solution of (
1
) on
\mathbb{T}_{0}
.
It is clear that the dissipation inequality

V\big(t_{2},x(t_{2})\big)-V\big(t_{1},x(t_{1})\big)\leq\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\realPart\big({y(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}u(t)\big)\,\mathrm{d}t

holds for all
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathbb{T}_{0}
such that
t_{1}\leq t_{2}<t_{0}
or
t_{0}\leq t_{1}\leq t_{2}
, since in those cases it reduces to the dissipation inequality for
V_{1}
or
V_{2}
, respectively.
Suppose now that
t_{1}<t_{0}\leq t_{2}
, then

\displaystyle V\big(t_{2},x(t_{2})\big)-V\big(t_{1},x(t_{1})\big)=V_{2}\big(t_{2},x(t_{2})\big)-V_{2}\big(t_{0},x(t_{0})\big)+V_{1}\big(t_{0},x(t_{0})\big)-V_{1}\big(t_{1},x(t_{1})\big)\leq

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{2}}\realPart\big({y(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}u(t)\big)\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{0}}\realPart\big({y(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}u(t)\big)\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\realPart\big({y(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}u(t)\big)\,\mathrm{d}t,

since
V_{1}(t_{0},\cdot)=V_{2}(t_{0},\cdot)
. We then conclude that
V
is a storage function on
\mathbb{T}_{0}
.
∎

We now proceed to extend the aforementioned result of
[
2
]
to our setting.

Theorem 5.14
.

Consider a passive LTV system of the form (
1
)
and suppose that for every
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathbb{T},\ t_{0}\leq t_{1}
there exists
c>0
such that
D(t)+{D(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}\geq cI_{m}
for a.e.
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
. Then the Riccati differential equation (
35
) has a solution
Q_{a}\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
which induces the available storage function
V_{a}
, i.e., such that
V_{a}=V_{Q_{a}}
.
In particular,
Q_{a}
is a solution of the differential
(KYP)
inequality (
4
).

Proof.

Note that the condition on
D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}}
implies that
D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}}\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{m}_{++}(\mathbb{C}))
and
(D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}})^{-1}\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{m}_{++}(\mathbb{C}))
.
Thus, applying the Schur complement, we can write

\begin{bmatrix}-{A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q-QA-\dot{Q}-({C}^{\mathrm{H}}-QB)(D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}})^{-1}(C-{B}^{\mathrm{H}}Q)&0\\
0&D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}}\end{bmatrix}\\
={\begin{bmatrix}I_{n}&0\\
-(D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}})^{-1}(C-{B}^{\mathrm{H}}Q)&I_{m}\end{bmatrix}}^{\mathrm{H}}\begin{bmatrix}-{A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q-QA-\dot{Q}&{C}^{\mathrm{H}}-QB\\
C-{B}^{\mathrm{H}}Q&D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}I_{n}&0\\
-(D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}})^{-1}(C-{B}^{\mathrm{H}}Q)&I_{m}\end{bmatrix}

a.e. in
\mathbb{T}
.
In particular, we deduce from
Corollary

5.6
that
Q\in\AUC_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
induces a storage function
V_{Q}
for (
1
) if and only if the Riccati differential inequality

{A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q+QA+\dot{Q}+({C}^{\mathrm{H}}-QB)(D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}})^{-1}(C-{B}^{\mathrm{H}}Q)\leq 0

holds a.e. on
\mathbb{T}
, or equivalently

{\widetilde{A}}^{\mathrm{H}}Q+Q\widetilde{A}+\dot{Q}+Q\widetilde{B}Q+\widetilde{C}\leq 0,

(36)

where

\widetilde{A}\coloneqq A-B(D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}})^{-1}C\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n,n})
,

\widetilde{B}\coloneqq B(D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}})^{-1}{B}^{\mathrm{H}}\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
and

\widetilde{C}\coloneqq{C}^{\mathrm{H}}(D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}})^{-1}C\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
.
We consider analogously the Riccati differential equation (
35
)

{\widetilde{A}}^{\mathrm{H}}Q+Q\widetilde{A}+\dot{Q}+Q\widetilde{B}Q+\widetilde{C}=0.

(37)

Let
t_{0}\in\mathbb{T}
be any time point, and let
Q\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T}_{1},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}(\mathbb{C}))
be the unique local solution of (
37
) satisfying
Q(t_{0})=Q_{a}(t_{0})
, where
\mathbb{T}_{1}=(t_{-1},t_{1})\subseteq\mathbb{T}
, with
-\infty\leq t_{-1}<t_{0}<t_{1}\leq+\infty
, is its maximum interval of definition.
In particular,

Q_{a}(t_{0})-Q(t)=-\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\dot{Q}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s=\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\big({\widetilde{A}}^{\mathrm{H}}Q+Q\widetilde{A}+Q\widetilde{B}Q+\widetilde{C}\big)\,\mathrm{d}s

holds for all
t\in\mathbb{T}_{1}
.
Let us rewrite (
37
) as

\dot{Q}=-{\widetilde{A}}^{\mathrm{H}}Q-Q\widetilde{A}-\widehat{C}

with
\widehat{C}\coloneqq Q\widetilde{B}Q+\widetilde{C}\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
.
Because of
Theorem

B.8
, we can then write

Q(t)=\Phi_{-\widetilde{A}}(t,t_{0})Q_{a}(t_{0}){\Phi_{-\widetilde{A}}(t,t_{0})}^{\mathrm{H}}-\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\Phi_{-\widetilde{A}}(t,s)\widehat{C}(s){\Phi_{-\widetilde{A}}(t,s)}^{\mathrm{H}}\,\mathrm{d}s\geq 0

for every
t\in(t_{-1},t_{0})
. In particular, we deduce that
V_{Q}
is a storage function for (
1
) on the restricted time interval
(t_{-1},t_{0}]\subseteq\mathbb{T}
, since
Q
satisfies (
36
).

Let us now define

\widetilde{D}:\mathbb{T}_{1}\to\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}(\mathbb{C}),\qquad t\mapsto Q_{a}(t)-Q_{a}(t_{0})+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\big({\widetilde{A}}^{\mathrm{H}}Q_{a}+Q_{a}\widetilde{A}+Q_{a}\widetilde{B}Q_{a}+\widetilde{C}\big)\,\mathrm{d}s,

in particular
\widetilde{D}\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T}_{1},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}(\mathbb{C}))
,
\widetilde{D}(t_{0})=0
, and

\displaystyle\widetilde{D}(s)-\widetilde{D}(r)

\displaystyle=Q_{a}(s)-Q_{a}(r)+\int_{r}^{s}\big({\widetilde{A}}^{\mathrm{H}}Q_{a}+Q_{a}\widetilde{A}+Q_{a}\widetilde{B}Q_{a}+\widetilde{C}\big)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\int_{r}^{s}\big(\dot{Q}_{a}+{\widetilde{A}}^{\mathrm{H}}Q_{a}+Q_{a}\widetilde{A}+Q_{a}\widetilde{B}Q_{a}+\widetilde{C}\big)\,\mathrm{d}s\leq 0,

for all
r,s\in\mathbb{T}_{1},\ r\leq s
, since
Q_{a}\in\AUC_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
solves (
36
), i.e.,
\widetilde{D}
is weakly decreasing.
By defining
\widetilde{Q}\coloneqq Q_{a}-Q\in\AUC_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}(\mathbb{C}))
we have that
\widetilde{Q}(t_{0})=0
and

\displaystyle\widetilde{Q}(t)

\displaystyle=Q_{a}(t)-Q(t)=\widetilde{D}(t)+Q_{a}(t_{0})-Q(t)-\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\big({\widetilde{A}}^{\mathrm{H}}Q_{a}+Q_{a}\widetilde{A}+Q_{a}\widetilde{B}Q_{a}+\widetilde{C}\big)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\widetilde{D}(t)+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\big({\widetilde{A}}^{\mathrm{H}}Q+Q\widetilde{A}+Q\widetilde{B}Q-{\widetilde{A}}^{\mathrm{H}}Q_{a}-Q_{a}\widetilde{A}-Q_{a}\widetilde{B}Q_{a}\big)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\widetilde{D}(t)+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\big(-{\widetilde{A}}^{\mathrm{H}}\widetilde{Q}-\widetilde{Q}\widetilde{A}-\widetilde{Q}\widetilde{B}\widetilde{Q}-\widetilde{Q}\widetilde{B}Q-Q\widetilde{B}\widetilde{Q}\big)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\widetilde{Q}(t_{0})+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\big(-{\widehat{A}}^{\mathrm{H}}\widetilde{Q}-\widetilde{Q}\widehat{A}-\widehat{C}\big)\,\mathrm{d}s+\widetilde{D}(t)-\widetilde{D}(t_{0}),

with
\widehat{A}\coloneqq\widetilde{A}+\widetilde{B}Q\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T}_{1},\mathbb{C}^{n,n})
and
\widehat{C}\coloneqq\widetilde{Q}\widetilde{B}\widetilde{Q}\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T}_{1},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
. Because of
Theorem

D.15
, we can then write

\widetilde{Q}(t)=-\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\Phi_{-{\widehat{A}}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,s)\widehat{C}(s){\Phi_{-{\widehat{A}}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,s)}^{\mathrm{H}}\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\left(\Phi_{-{\widehat{A}}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,s)\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{D}(s){\Phi_{-{\widehat{A}}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,s)}^{\mathrm{H}}\right)

for all
t\in\mathbb{T}_{1}
, where
\Phi_{-{\widehat{A}}^{\mathrm{H}}}\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T}_{1},\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
denotes the state-transition matrix associated to
\dot{x}=-{\widehat{A}}^{\mathrm{H}}x
.
In particular, for
t\leq t_{0}
we have
\widetilde{Q}(t)\geq 0
, since
\widehat{C}
is positive semi-definite a.e. on
\mathbb{T}_{1}
and
\widetilde{D}
is decreasing, see
Corollary

D.17
.
Thus,
0\leq Q(t)\leq Q_{a}(t)
for all
t\in(t_{-1},t_{0}]
.

Suppose now for the sake of contradiction that
t_{-1}>\inf(\mathbb{T})
, in particular
t_{-1}\in\mathbb{R}
.
Since
0\leq Q(t)\leq Q_{a}(t)
for all
t\in(t_{-1},t_{0}]
, and
Q\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{T}_{1},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))\subseteq W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T}_{1},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
, we deduce that
Q(t_{-1})=\lim_{t\to t_{-1}^{+}}Q(t)
exists and satisfies
0\leq Q(t_{-1})\leq\lVert Q_{a}\rVert_{L^{\infty}([t_{-1},t_{0}])}\cdot I_{n}<\infty
. Then we can extend
Q
locally from
(t_{-1},Q(t_{-1}))
and obtain a larger defining interval for
Q
, which contradicts the maximality of
\mathbb{T}_{1}
.
We conclude that necessarily
t_{-1}=\inf(\mathbb{T})
, i.e., that
\mathbb{T}
and
\mathbb{T}_{1}
have the same left end point.

Let us then define

\widehat{Q}(t):\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{C}^{n,n},\qquad t\mapsto\begin{cases}Q(t)&\text{for }t<t_{0},\\
Q_{a}(t)&\text{for }t\geq t_{0}.\end{cases}

It is clear by construction that
\widehat{Q}\in\AUC_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
with
\widehat{Q}\leq Q_{a}
pointwise.
Furthermore, since
Q
and
Q_{a}
define storage functions for (
1
) restricted on
(t_{-1},t_{0}]
and
\mathbb{T}
, respectively, and
Q(t_{0})=Q_{a}(t_{0})
, we deduce from
Lemma

5.13
that
\widehat{Q}
defines a storage function on
\mathbb{T}
.
In particular,
\widehat{Q}
defines a smaller storage function than
Q_{a}
.
However, since
Q_{a}
is minimal, this can only be the case if
\widehat{Q}=Q_{a}
.
Due to
Theorem

5.12
, we conclude that
Q_{a}
is a solution of the differential KYP inequality (
4
).
∎

Remark 5.15
.

Note that if we have the condition
D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}}\geq cI_{m}
as in
Theorem

5.14
, then we can modify
Figure

1
to equivalently have the Riccati inequality instead of
(KYP)
.

Remark 5.16
.

There is a close connection between the Riccati differential equation and the matrix differential-algebraic equation

\begin{bmatrix}\dot{X}\\
-\dot{Y}\\
0\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}0&A&B\\
{A}^{\mathrm{H}}&0&-{C}^{\mathrm{H}}\\
{B}^{\mathrm{H}}&-C&-D-{D}^{\mathrm{H}}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}Y\\
X\\
U\end{bmatrix},

(38)

with the extra condition
Y=QX
.
The condition that
D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}}\geq cI_{m}
, in particular, implies that this differential algebraic equation is of differentiation index one, see
[
32
]
, and by taking the Schur complement one gets to the matrix Riccati differential equation

\big(\dot{Q}+{A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q+QA+({C}^{\mathrm{H}}-QB)(D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}})^{-1}(C-{B}^{\mathrm{H}}Q)\big)X=0,

which for pointwise invertible
X\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n,n})
implies that
Q_{a}=Q=YX^{-1}
, for which the solution theory is discussed in
[
1
,
47
]
.

Note that many of the properties of (
38
) still hold if
D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}}
is indefinite, see
[
37
,
49
]
but then typically the system is not equivalent to a
(pH)
system; see
[
15
]
in the LTI case and
Theorem

6.8
for the LTV case.

6
Nonnegative supply and its implications for pH systems

In this section, we study the properties of LTV systems with nonnegative supply
(NN)
, and in particular what this property implies in terms of passivity
(Pa)
, solvability of the
(KYP)
inequality, and admitting a
(pH)
representation.

6.1
Nonnegativity of the Popov operator

For LTI systems, the nonnegativity of the Popov operator is equivalent to the positive realness of the transfer function, see
[
35
, Proposition 2.36]
. In this subsection, we define the Popov and transfer operators for LTV systems and study their properties and their relation to each other. These operators can be considered as time-domain counterparts of the Popov function and the transfer function, respectively.

Definition 6.1
.

For all
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathbb{T},\ t_{0}\leq t_{1}
we call

\begin{split}&\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t_{0},t_{1}}:L^{2}\big([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{m}\big)\to L^{2}\big([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{m}\big),\qquad u\mapsto\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t_{0},t_{1}}u,\\
&\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t_{0},t_{1}}u(t)\coloneqq\int_{t_{0}}^{t}C(t)\Phi(t,s)B(s)u(s)\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{t}^{t_{1}}{B(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}{\Phi(s,t)}^{\mathrm{H}}{C(s)}^{\mathrm{H}}u(s)\,\mathrm{d}s+\big(D(t)+{D(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}\big)u(t).\end{split}

(39)

the
Popov operator
of the system (
1
) in
(t_{0},t_{1})
.

We first verify that the Popov operator is well defined.

Lemma 6.2
.

For all
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathbb{T},\ t_{0}\leq t_{1}
, the Popov operator
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t_{0},t_{1}}
as in (
39
) is a well-defined bounded linear operator.

Proof.

For the proof, we refer to
Lemma

A.6
.
∎

Remark 6.3
.

Note that
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t_{0},t_{1}}
is also well defined as an operator on
L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m})
. However, the properties that make this operator of interest only concern the fixed compact interval
[t_{0},t_{1}]\subseteq\mathbb{T}
.
Furthermore, since
L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m})
is not a normed vector space, the boundedness of
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t_{0},t_{1}}
is lost in that setting.
Note that the dependency of
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t_{0},t_{1}}
on
t_{0},t_{1}
cannot be removed, as these parameters determine the integration intervals.

By interpreting the general state solution in terms of the initial condition
x(t_{0})=x_{0}
for
(t_{0},x_{0})\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}
and of the state transition matrix using (
8
), we write the output trajectory in the form of

\displaystyle y(t)

\displaystyle=C(t)\Phi(t,t_{0})x_{0}+C(t)\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\Phi(t,s)B(s)u(s)\,\mathrm{d}s+D(t)u(t)

\displaystyle=C(t)\Phi(t,t_{0})x_{0}+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}C(t)\Phi(t,s)B(s)u(s)\,\mathrm{d}s+D(t)u(t)

(40)

for a.e.
t\in\mathbb{T}
. We express (
40
) as

y(t)=C(t)\Phi(t,t_{0})x_{0}+\mathbf{Z}_{t_{0}}u(t),

(41)

where
\mathbf{Z}_{t_{0}}:L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m})\to L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m})
is the
transfer operator
at
t_{0}\in\mathbb{T}
, defined such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{Z}_{t_{0}}u(t)\coloneqq\int_{t_{0}}^{t}C(t)\Phi(t,s)B(s)u(s)\,\mathrm{d}s+D(t)u(t),

(42)

for all
t\in\mathbb{T}
.
The transfer operator is clearly a linear operator on
L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m})
.

Consider now a compact subinterval
[t_{0},t_{1}]\subseteq\mathbb{T}
: On the one hand, it is clear that for every input
u\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m})
, the restriction
(\mathbf{Z}_{t_{0}}u)|_{[t_{0},t_{1}]}\in L^{2}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{m})
depends only on
u|_{[t_{0},t_{1}]}\in L^{2}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{m})
.
On the other hand, for every local input
\widetilde{u}\in L^{2}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{m})
we can trivially extend
\widetilde{u}
to

\underline{\widetilde{u}}:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{C}^{m},\qquad t\mapsto\begin{cases}\widetilde{u}(t)&\text{for }t\in[t_{0},t_{1}],\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

which clearly satisfies
\underline{\widetilde{u}}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m})
.
Therefore,
\mathbf{Z}_{t_{0}}
induces a
local transfer operator

\mathbf{Z}_{t_{0},t_{1}}
on
L^{2}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{m})
that satisfies

\mathbf{Z}_{t_{0},t_{1}}\widetilde{u}(t)=\int_{t_{0}}^{t}C(t)\Phi(t,s)B(s)\widetilde{u}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s+D(t)\widetilde{u}(t)=(\mathbf{Z}_{t_{0}}\underline{\widetilde{u}})|_{[t_{0},t_{1}]}

(43)

for all
\widetilde{u}\in L^{2}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{m})
and
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
.
In particular, we have the following result.

Lemma 6.4
.

For every
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathbb{T},\ t_{0}\leq t_{1}
, the local transfer operator
\mathbf{Z}_{t_{0},t_{1}}
in (
43
) is a well-defined and bounded linear operator.
Furthermore, the formula

y(t)=C(t)\Phi(t,t_{0})x_{0}+(\mathbf{Z}_{t_{0},t_{1}}u|_{[t_{0},t_{1}]})(t)

(44)

holds for all
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
and for every state-input-output solution
(x,u,y)
of (
1
).

Proof.

For the proof that
\mathbf{Z}_{t_{0},t_{1}}
is well-defined, bounded and linear, we refer to
Lemma

A.7
.
Furthermore, since

(\mathbf{Z}_{t_{0},t_{1}}u|_{[t_{0},t_{1}]})(t)=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}C(t)\Phi(t,s)B(s)u(s)\,\mathrm{d}s+D(t)u(t)=\mathbf{Z}_{t_{0}}u(t)

holds for all
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
, the formula (
44
) follows from (
41
).
∎

We now analyze the relation between the Popov and the transfer operator, which is analogous to the one between the Popov and the transfer function in the LTI case.

Theorem 6.5
.

Consider a LTV system of the form (
1
) on
\mathbb{T}
with the transfer operator
\mathbf{Z}_{t_{0},t_{1}}
in the Hilbert space
L^{2}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{m})
given by (
42
) and Popov operator
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t_{0},t_{1}}
given in
Definition

6.1
, with
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathbb{T},\ t_{0}\leq t_{1}
. Then the following statements hold.

(a)

The adjoint
{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathrm{H}}_{t_{0},t_{1}}
of the transfer operator
\mathbf{Z}_{t_{0},t_{1}}
is given by

{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathrm{H}}_{t_{0},t_{1}}u(t):=\int_{t}^{t_{1}}{B(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}{\Phi(s,t)}^{\mathrm{H}}{C}^{\mathrm{H}}(s)u(s)\,\mathrm{d}s+{D(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}u(t)\quad\text{for }t_{0}\leq t\leq t_{1}.

(b)

The Popov operator is given by
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{t_{0},t_{1}}=\mathbf{Z}_{t_{0},t_{1}}+{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathrm{H}}_{t_{0},t_{1}}
. In particular,
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{t_{0},t_{1}}
is self-adjoint.

Furthermore,

\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\realPart\big({y(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}u(t)\big)\,\mathrm{d}t=\realPart\left(\left(\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}{u(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}C(t)\Phi(t,t_{0})\,\mathrm{d}t\right)x_{0}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\langle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t_{0},t_{1}}u|_{[t_{0},t_{1}]},u|_{[t_{0},t_{1}]}\rangle

(45)

holds for every state-input-output solution
(x,u,y)
of (
1
).

Proof.

Observe that
\mathbf{Z}_{t_{0},t_{1}}=\mathbf{Z}_{1}+\mathbf{Z}_{0}
with

\mathbf{Z}_{1}u(t)=\int_{t_{0}}^{t}C(t)\Phi(t,s)B(s)u(s)\,\mathrm{d}s,\qquad\mathbf{Z}_{0}u(t)=D(t)u(t)

Then

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{Z}_{1}u_{2},u_{1}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}{u_{1}(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}C(t)\Phi(t,s)B(s)u_{2}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}{\left(\int_{s}^{t_{1}}{B(s)}^{\mathrm{H}}{\Phi(t,s)}^{\mathrm{H}}{C(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}u_{1}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\right)}^{\mathrm{H}}u_{2}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s=\langle u_{2},{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathrm{H}}_{1}u_{1}\rangle,

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{Z}_{0}u_{2},u_{1}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}{u_{1}(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}D(t)u_{2}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}{\big({D(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}u_{2}(t)\big)}^{\mathrm{H}}u_{1}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=\langle u_{2},{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathrm{H}}_{0}u_{1}\rangle,

where

\displaystyle{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathrm{H}}_{1}u(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{t}^{t_{1}}{B(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}{\Phi(s,t)}^{\mathrm{H}}{C(s)}^{\mathrm{H}}u(s)\,\mathrm{d}s,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathrm{H}}_{0}u(t)

\displaystyle={D(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}u(t).

It follows that

\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t_{0},t_{1}}u(t)=(\mathbf{Z}_{t_{0},t_{1}}+{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathrm{H}}_{t_{0},t_{1}})u(t).

This implies that
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t_{0},t_{1}}
is self-adjoint. Moreover, from (
44
) we obtain that

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\realPart\big({y(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}u(t)\big)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\realPart\left(\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}{u(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}\big(C(t)\Phi(t,t_{0})x_{0}+(\mathbf{Z}_{t_{0},t_{1}}u|_{[t_{0},t_{1}]})(t)\big)\,\mathrm{d}t\right)

\displaystyle=\realPart\left(\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\left({u(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}C(t)\Phi(t,t_{0})\,\mathrm{d}t\right)x_{0}\right)+\realPart(\langle\mathbf{Z}_{t_{0},t_{1}}u|_{[t_{0},t_{1}]},u|_{[t_{0},t_{1}]}\rangle)

\displaystyle=\realPart\left(\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\left({u(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}C(t)\Phi(t,t_{0})\,\mathrm{d}t\right)x_{0}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\langle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t_{0},t_{1}}u|_{[t_{0},t_{1}]},u|_{[t_{0},t_{1}]}\rangle.\qed

Since the first term in (
45
) vanishes for
x_{0}=0
, by
Theorem

6.5
, we immediately have the following result.

Corollary 6.6
.

The supply of an LTV system (
1
) satisfies

\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\realPart\big({y(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}u(t)\big)\,\mathrm{d}t=\frac{1}{2}\langle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t_{0},t_{1}}u,u\rangle_{L^{2}}

(46)

for every
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathbb{T},\ t_{0}\leq t_{1}
and every state-input-output solution
(x,u,y)
of (
1
) such that
x(t_{0})=0
.
In particular, the system (
1
) has nonnegative supply
(NN)
if and only if the Popov operator
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t_{0},t_{1}}
is positive semi-definite for all
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathbb{T},\ t_{0}\leq t_{1}
.

Corollary

6.6
introduces a condition equivalent to
(NN)
in terms of the positive semi-definiteness of the Popov operator, which resembles the known definition of positive realness for LTI systems; see, e.g.
[
13
]
. In
[
35
]
,
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
is defined as the integral of a kernel involving the Heaviside step function and the Dirac delta distribution. Similarly, the transfer function
\mathbf{Z}_{t_{0}}
can be equivalently defined as the integral of a kernel, which was introduced in
[
41
]
under the name
impulse response matrix
and in
[
2
]
as
impedance matrix
.

Remark 6.7
.

By only looking at (
46
), one might be tempted to claim that
y=\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t_{0},t_{1}}u
for
x(t_{0})=0
.
However, this is in general not true, unless quite restrictive assumptions like
C(t)\Phi(t,s)B(s)=0
and
D(t)={D(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}
for all
t,s\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
hold. In fact, Consider the example

\dot{x}=-x+u,\qquad y=x,

with
x(t),u(t),y(t)\in\mathbb{C}
on
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{R}
, which is clearly
(pH)
with Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}(x)=\frac{1}{2}\lvert x\rvert^{2}
, and observe that
y=\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t_{0},t_{1}}u
for
x(t_{0})=0
would imply

\int_{t_{0}}^{t}e^{t-s}u(s)\,\mathrm{d}s=\frac{1}{2}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}e^{t-s}u(s)\,\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{2}\int_{t}^{t_{1}}e^{s-t}u(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\implies\int_{t_{0}}^{t}e^{t-s}u(s)\,\mathrm{d}s=\int_{t}^{t_{1}}e^{s-t}u(s)\,\mathrm{d}s

for every
u\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
and a.e.
t\in\mathbb{T}
, which is evidently not true.

6.2
Relation of nonnegative supply and passivity

In this subsection, we study the property of having nonnegative supply
(NN)
and its relation to passivity.

In
Theorem

5.14
we have assumed that
D(t)+{D(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}\geq cI_{m}
in every compact subinterval, which in turn implies the weaker condition
D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}}\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{m}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
, which is well-known to be necessary for the passivity of the system. The next results show that this condition is also necessary for an LTV system to have a nonnegative supply.

Theorem 6.8
.

If an LTV system of the from (
1
) has nonnegative supply
(NN)
, then
D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}}\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{m}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
.

Proof.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that
D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}}\notin L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{m}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
.
By
Theorem

4.2
there exist
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathbb{T}
and
t_{0}\leq t_{1}
, and
u_{0}\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
with
\lVert u_{0}\rVert_{2}=1
satisfying

M\coloneqq-{u_{0}}^{\mathrm{H}}\left(\int^{t_{1}}_{t_{0}}\big(D(t)+{D}^{\mathrm{H}}(t)\big)\,\mathrm{d}t\right)u_{0}>0.

By a bisection procedure we construct a sequence of intervals
\{{\mathbb{T}}_{k}\mid k\in\mathbb{N}\}
such that
\mathbb{T}_{0}=[t_{0},t_{1}]
,
\mathbb{T}_{k+1}\subseteq\mathbb{T}_{k}=[t_{0}^{k},t_{1}^{k}]
and
|\mathbb{T}_{k}|=2^{-k}|\mathbb{T}_{0}|=2^{-k}(t_{1}-t_{0})
with

\int_{\mathbb{T}_{k}}{u}^{\mathrm{H}}\big(D(t)+{D}^{\mathrm{H}}(t)\big)u\,\mathrm{d}t\leq-2^{-k}M<0.

Let
\varepsilon>0
be an arbitrarily small number, that we will select later. By
Corollary

A.3
the inequalities

\int_{\mathbb{T}_{k}}\lVert B(t)\rVert_{2}\,\mathrm{d}t<2^{-\frac{k}{2}}\varepsilon,\quad\int_{\mathbb{T}_{k}}\lVert C(t)\rVert_{2}\,\mathrm{d}t<2^{-\frac{k}{2}}\varepsilon

then hold for sufficiently large
k
, since
B,C\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}
.
Let now
u\equiv u_{0}\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m})
be a constant input and let
(x,u,y)
be the state-input-output solution associated with
x(t_{0}^{k})=0
, for such large
k
. Due to
Corollary

6.6
it holds that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{\mathbb{T}_{k}}\realPart\big({y(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}u(t)\big)\,\mathrm{d}t=\langle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t_{0}^{k},t_{1}^{k}}u,u\rangle_{L^{2}}=\int_{\mathbb{T}_{k}}{u(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}\Bigg(\int_{t_{0}^{k}}^{t}C(t)\Phi(t,s)B(s)u(s)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\int_{t}^{t_{1}^{k}}{B(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}{\Phi(s,t)}^{\mathrm{H}}{C(s)}^{\mathrm{H}}u(s)\,\mathrm{d}s+\big(D(t)+{D(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}\big)u(t)\Bigg)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{T}_{k}}\int_{\mathbb{T}_{k}}\left\lvert{u_{0}}^{\mathrm{H}}C(t)X(t)X^{-1}(s)B(s)u_{0}\right\rvert\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\int_{\mathbb{T}_{k}}\int_{\mathbb{T}_{k}}\left\lvert{u_{0}}^{\mathrm{H}}{B(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}{X(t)}^{-\mathrm{H}}{X(s)}^{\mathrm{H}}{C(s)}^{\mathrm{H}}u_{0}\right\rvert\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}t+{u_{0}}^{\mathrm{H}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}_{k}}\big(D(t)+{D(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}\big)\,\mathrm{d}t\right)u_{0}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{T}_{k}}\int_{\mathbb{T}_{k}}\lVert C(t)\rVert\lVert X(t)\rVert\lVert X(s)^{-1}\rVert\lVert B(s)\rVert\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\int_{\mathbb{T}_{k}}\int_{\mathbb{T}_{k}}\lVert B(t)\rVert\lVert X(t)^{-1}\rVert\lVert X(s)\rVert\lVert C(s)\rVert\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}t-2^{-k}M

\displaystyle\leq 2\lVert X\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert X^{-1}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\int_{\mathbb{T}_{k}}\lVert C(t)\rVert\,\mathrm{d}t\int_{\mathbb{T}_{k}}\lVert B(s)\rVert\,\mathrm{d}s-2^{-k}M

\displaystyle\leq 2^{1-k}\varepsilon\lVert X\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert X^{-1}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}-2^{-k}M=2^{-k}\left(2\varepsilon\lVert X\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert X^{-1}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}-M\right),

where the
L^{\infty}
norm of
X
and
X^{-1}
is taken with respect to the interval
[t_{0},t_{1}]
.
Then, by selecting

\varepsilon<\frac{M}{2\lVert X\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert X^{-1}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}}

and
k
sufficiently large, we obtain the contradiction
0<0
which implies that the
D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}}\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{m}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
is necessary.
∎

Note that, by
Theorem

3.5
and
Theorem

6.8
, it follows that
D+D\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{m}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
is also a necessary condition for
(pH)
,
(KYP)
and
(Pa)
to be fulfilled.

In
[
35
, Theorem 4.105]
, it was proven that, for completely reachable LTV systems,
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t_{0},t_{1}}
is nonnegative if and only if the system is passive. In
[
26
, Theorem 4]
it was shown for real-valued LTV systems that a system has nonnegative supply if and only if a storage function can be defined in all reachable states. To be consistent with our setting, we present an alternative proof. For this, we need the following two lemmas.

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
t_{-1},t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathbb{T},\ t_{-1}\leq t_{0}\leq t_{1}
and let
(x,u,y)
be a state-input-output solution of the LTV system (
1
) such that
x(t_{-1})=0
.
Then the available storage function satisfies

V_{a}\big(t_{1},x(t_{1})\big)\leq V_{a}\big(t_{0},x(t_{0})\big)+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\realPart\big({y(s)}^{\mathrm{H}}u(s)\big)\,\mathrm{d}s.

(47)

In particular, if
V_{a}(t_{0},x(t_{0}))
is finite, then
V_{a}(t_{1},x(t_{1}))
is also finite.

Proof.

Let
x_{0}=x(t_{0})
, let
x_{1}=x(t_{1})
, and let us consider the restricted input space

\mathcal{U}=\{\widetilde{u}\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m})\mid\widetilde{u}(t)=u(t)\text{ for a.e.\penalty\ }t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]\}.

If we denote by
(\widetilde{x},\widetilde{u},\widetilde{y})
any state-input-output solution with
\widetilde{x}(t_{0})=x_{0}
and
\widetilde{u}\in\mathcal{U}
, then we observe that
(x,u,y)=(\widetilde{x},\widetilde{u},\widetilde{y})
a.e. on
[t_{0},t_{1}]
, in particular
(\widetilde{x},\widetilde{y})
are alternatively determined by
\widetilde{x}(t_{1})=x_{1}
.
Furthermore, for
t>t_{1}
, the output
\widetilde{y}(t)
depends only on the values of
\widetilde{u}(t)
for
t>t_{1}
. Then we have that

\displaystyle V_{a}\big(t_{0},x(t_{0})\big)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}(t_{2},\widetilde{u})\,:\,t_{2}\geq t_{0},\\
\widetilde{x}(t_{0})=x_{0}\end{subarray}{}}\left(-\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{2}}\realPart({\widetilde{y}(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}\widetilde{u}(t))\,\mathrm{d}t\right)

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\mathcal{U},\ t_{2}\geq t_{1},\\
\widetilde{x}(t_{0})=x_{0}\end{subarray}}\left(-\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{2}}\realPart({\widetilde{y}(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}\widetilde{u}(t))\,\mathrm{d}t\right)

\displaystyle=-\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\realPart({y(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}u(t))\,\mathrm{d}t+\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}(t_{2},\widetilde{u})\,:\,t_{2}\geq t_{1},\\
\widetilde{x}(t_{1})=x_{1}\end{subarray}{}}\left(-\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{2}}\realPart({\widetilde{y}(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}\widetilde{u}(t))\,\mathrm{d}t\right)

\displaystyle\leq-\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\realPart\big({y(s)}^{\mathrm{H}}u(s)\big)\,\mathrm{d}s+V_{a}\big(t_{1},x(t_{1})\big),

which implies (
47
).
Suppose now that
V_{a}(t_{0},x(t_{0}))
is finite. Since the supply is finite, we immediately deduce from (
47
) that
V_{a}(t_{1},x(t_{1}))
is also finite.
∎

Lemma 6.10
.

If the LTV system (
1
) has nonnegative supply
(NN)
, then its available storage satisfies
V_{a}(t,0)=0
for all
t\in\mathbb{T}
.

Proof.

Since the system has nonnegative supply, we have

0\leq V_{a}(t_{0},0)=-\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}(t,u)\,:\,t\geq t_{0},\\
x(t_{0})=0\end{subarray}}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\realPart\big({y(s)}^{\mathrm{H}}u(s)\big)\,\mathrm{d}s\leq 0,

implying that
V_{a}(t_{0},0)=0
for all
t_{0}\in\mathbb{T}
.
∎

We then have the following corollary.

Corollary 6.11
.

Consider an LTV system of the form (
1
). If the system has a nonnegative supply
(NN)
and is completely reachable, then the system is passive
(Pa)
.

Proof.

Since the system is completely reachable, every
(t_{0},x_{0})\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}
is reachable from some
(t_{-1},0)\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}
with
t_{-1}\leq t
.
Due to
Lemma

6.10
, we have
V_{a}(t_{-1},0)=0
, thus
V_{a}(t_{0},x_{0})
is finite, because of
Lemma

6.9
.
Then by
Theorem

5.2
the system is passive.
∎

Note that without complete reachability the nonnegativity of the Popov operator is not sufficient for passivity, see
[
13
, Example 5]
for a linear time-invariant example.

One may wonder whether it was really necessary to restrict the choice of
\mathbb{T}
to open intervals, since in control one often considers time intervals of the form
[0,T]
or
[0,+\infty)
.
While many properties (e.g. the existence of solutions) still hold when we replace an open interval
\mathbb{T}
with its closure, some of our results are lost.
The following example presents a passive LTV system defined on an open interval
\mathbb{T}
that is not passive when extended to
\overline{\mathbb{T}}
.

Example 6.12
.

Consider the LTV system

\dot{x}(t)=tu(t),\qquad y(t)=x(t),

for
t\in\mathbb{T}=(0,\infty)
and
x\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C})
. This system has continuously differentiable coefficients and is
(pH)
with
\mathcal{H}(t,x)=\frac{1}{2}{x}^{\mathrm{H}}Q(t)x
and
Q(t)=\frac{1}{t}
,
Q\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{1}_{++}(\mathbb{C}))
. By
Theorem

3.5
we also have properties
(KYP)
,
(Pa)
and
(NN)
.

Now consider the extended interval
\overline{\mathbb{T}}=[0,\infty)
.
Since the coefficients of the original system are continuously differentiable in
\overline{\mathbb{T}}
, it is clear that the system is well-posed and for every
x_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
and any admissible input
u\in L^{2}(\overline{\mathbb{T}},\mathbb{C})
, there exists a unique solution
x(t)=x(t_{0})+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}su(s)\,\mathrm{d}s
such that
x(t_{0})=x_{0}
,
t_{0}\in\overline{\mathbb{T}}
.
Furthermore, the system has nonnegative supply
(NN)
, since

\displaystyle 2\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\realPart\big({y(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}u(t)\big)\,\mathrm{d}t=2\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\realPart\Bigg({\bigg(\int_{t_{0}}^{t}su(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\bigg)}^{\mathrm{H}}u(s)\Bigg)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}s\overline{u(s)}u(t)\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\int_{t}^{t_{1}}t\overline{u(s)}u(t)\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\min(s,t)\overline{u(s)}u(t)\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\boldsymbol{1}(s-w)\boldsymbol{1}(t-w)\,\mathrm{d}w\,\overline{u(s)}u(t)\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\overline{\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}u(s)\boldsymbol{1}(s-w)\,\mathrm{d}s}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}u(t)\boldsymbol{1}(t-w)\,\mathrm{d}t\,\,\mathrm{d}w=\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\left\lvert\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}u(t)\boldsymbol{1}(t-w)\,\mathrm{d}t\right\rvert^{2}\geq 0.

Here we have used that

\min(s,t)=\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\boldsymbol{1}(s-w)\boldsymbol{1}(t-w)\,\mathrm{d}w,\quad\text{with }\boldsymbol{1}(t)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }t\geq 0\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Moreover, for all
t_{0}\in\overline{\mathbb{T}}
and all initial states
x_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
, the input
u(t)=\frac{3t}{t_{0}-t_{-1}}x_{0}
with
x(t_{-1})=0
,
t_{-1}\in\overline{\mathbb{T}}
and
t_{-1}\leq t_{0}
drives
(t_{-1},0)
to
(t_{0},x_{0})
. Therefore, this system is completely reachable.
However, the system is not passive, because the available storage satisfies
V_{a}(0,x_{0})=+\infty
for all
x_{0}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
.
This results from the fact that for every input of the form
u(t)=-\varepsilon^{-\tfrac{3}{2}}x_{0}\boldsymbol{1}(\varepsilon-t)
with
\varepsilon\geq 0
and every
t_{1}\geq\varepsilon
it holds that

-2\int_{0}^{t_{1}}{y(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}u(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=2\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\frac{x_{0}}{\varepsilon^{\tfrac{3}{2}}}\left(\overline{x}_{0}-\int_{0}^{t}s\frac{\overline{x}_{0}}{\varepsilon^{\tfrac{3}{2}}}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)\,\mathrm{d}t=\left(\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{\tfrac{1}{2}}}-\frac{1}{3}\right)\lvert x_{0}\rvert^{2}.

Since for
x_{0}\neq 0
the supremum for
\varepsilon>0
of this quantity is
+\infty
, we conclude that
V_{a}(0,x_{0})=+\infty
and the system is not passive.

6.3
The relationship between nonnegative supply and KYP inequality

Instead of going through
(Pa)
, there exists a direct relation between
(NN)
and
(KYP)
.
For
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathbb{T}
,
t_{0}\leq t_{1}
let us consider the map

\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}:L^{2}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{m})\to W^{1,1}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{n}),\qquad\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}u(t)\coloneqq\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\Phi(t,s)B(s)u(s)\,\mathrm{d}s,

which is bounded and linear. By (
8
)
\Psi
maps an input signal
u
to the corresponding solution
x
with initial condition
x(t_{0})=0
, in particular
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\circ\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}=A\circ\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}+B
and
\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}(\cdot)(t_{0})=0
hold.
For every
u\in L^{2}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{m})
and
z\in L^{2}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{n})
we have

\displaystyle\langle\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}u,z\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}{z(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}\Phi(t,s)B(s)u(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int^{t_{1}}_{t_{0}}\int^{t_{1}}_{s}{\left({B(s)}^{\mathrm{H}}{\Phi(t,s)}^{\mathrm{H}}z(t)\right)}^{\mathrm{H}}u(s)\,\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}{\left({B(s)}^{\mathrm{H}}\int^{t_{1}}_{s}{\Phi(t,s)}^{\mathrm{H}}z(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\right)}^{\mathrm{H}}u(s)\,\mathrm{d}s.

Therefore,
\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}
has the adjoint operator

{\Psi}^{\mathrm{H}}_{t_{0},t_{1}}:L^{2}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{n})\to L^{2}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{m}),\qquad{\Psi}^{\mathrm{H}}_{t_{0},t_{1}}z(s)\coloneqq{B(s)}^{\mathrm{H}}\int^{t_{1}}_{s}{\Phi(t,s)}^{\mathrm{H}}z(t)\,\mathrm{d}t

and we have the following lemma.

Lemma 6.13
.

Let
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathbb{T}
,
t_{0}\leq t_{1}
and
Q\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}(\mathbb{C}))
. Then it holds that

{\begin{bmatrix}\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}\\
\mathrm{Id}\end{bmatrix}}^{\mathrm{H}}\begin{bmatrix}-{A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q-QA-\dot{Q}&{C}^{\mathrm{H}}-QB\\
C-{B}^{\mathrm{H}}Q&D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}\\
\mathrm{Id}\end{bmatrix}=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t_{0},t_{1}}-\mathbf{R},

(48)

where

\mathbf{R}:L^{2}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{m})\to L^{2}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{m}),\qquad\mathbf{R}u(t)\coloneqq{B(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}{\Phi(t_{1},t)}^{\mathrm{H}}Q(t_{1})\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}u(t_{1})

is a self-adjoint bounded linear operator and
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t_{0},t_{1}}
is as defined in
Definition

6.1
. If
Q\geq 0
, then also
\mathbf{R}\geq 0
.

Proof.

The left-hand side of (
48
) can be written as

C\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}+{\Psi}^{\mathrm{H}}_{t_{0},t_{1}}{C}^{\mathrm{H}}+D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}}-{\Psi}^{\mathrm{H}}_{t_{0},t_{1}}{A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}-{\Psi}^{\mathrm{H}}_{t_{0},t_{1}}QA\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}-{\Psi}^{\mathrm{H}}_{t_{0},t_{1}}\dot{Q}\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}-{\Psi}^{\mathrm{H}}_{t_{0},t_{1}}QB-{B}^{\mathrm{H}}Q\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}.

Note that

\displaystyle\big(C\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}+{\Psi}^{\mathrm{H}}_{t_{0},t_{1}}{C}^{\mathrm{H}}+D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}}\big)u(t)

\displaystyle=C(t)\int^{t}_{t_{0}}\Phi(t,s)B(s)u(s)\,\mathrm{d}s+{B(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}\int^{t_{1}}_{t}{\Phi(s,t)}^{\mathrm{H}}{C(s)}^{\mathrm{H}}u(s)\,\mathrm{d}s+\big(D(t)+{D(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}\big)u(t)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Z}_{t_{0},t_{1}}u(t)+{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathrm{H}}_{t_{0},t_{1}}u(t)r=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t_{0},t_{1}}u(t)

holds for all
u\in L^{2}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{m})
and
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
, i.e.,

C\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}+{\Psi}^{\mathrm{H}}_{t_{0},t_{1}}{C}^{\mathrm{H}}+D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}}=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t_{0},t_{1}}
.
Furthermore, using integration by parts and (
6
), i.e.
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Phi(t,s)=A(t)\Phi(t,s)
we obtain

\displaystyle{\Psi}^{\mathrm{H}}_{t_{0},t_{1}}\dot{Q}\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}u(s)={B(s)}^{\mathrm{H}}\int_{s}^{t_{1}}{\Phi(t,s)}^{\mathrm{H}}\dot{Q}(t)\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}u(t)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\qquad={B(s)}^{\mathrm{H}}\left({\Phi(t_{1},s)}^{\mathrm{H}}Q(t_{1})\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}u(t_{1})-Q(s)\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}u(s)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-{B(s)}^{\mathrm{H}}\left(\int_{s}^{t_{1}}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\Phi(t,s)}^{\mathrm{H}}Q(t)\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}u(t)\,\mathrm{d}t-\int_{s}^{t_{1}}{\Phi(t,s)}^{\mathrm{H}}Q(t)\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}u(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathbf{R}u(s)-{B}^{\mathrm{H}}Q\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}u(s)-\int_{s}^{t_{1}}{\Phi(t,s)}^{\mathrm{H}}{A(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}Q(t)\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}u(t)\,\mathrm{d}t-{\Psi}^{\mathrm{H}}_{t_{0},t_{1}}\big(Q(A\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}u+Bu)\big)(s)

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathbf{R}u(s)-{B}^{\mathrm{H}}Q\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}u(s)-{\Psi}^{\mathrm{H}}_{t_{0},t_{1}}{A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}u(s)-{\Psi}^{\mathrm{H}}_{t_{0},t_{1}}QA\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}u(s)-{\Psi}^{\mathrm{H}}_{t_{0},t_{1}}QBu(s)

\displaystyle\qquad=(\mathbf{R}-{B}^{\mathrm{H}}Q\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}-{\Psi}^{\mathrm{H}}_{t_{0},t_{1}}{A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}-{\Psi}^{\mathrm{H}}_{t_{0},t_{1}}QA\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}-{\Psi}^{\mathrm{H}}_{t_{0},t_{1}}QB)u(s)

for all
u\in L^{2}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{m})
and
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
, i.e.,

{\Psi}^{\mathrm{H}}_{t_{0},t_{1}}{A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}+{\Psi}^{\mathrm{H}}_{t_{0},t_{1}}QA\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}+{\Psi}^{\mathrm{H}}_{t_{0},t_{1}}\dot{Q}\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}+{\Psi}^{\mathrm{H}}_{t_{0},t_{1}}QB+{B}^{\mathrm{H}}Q\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}=\mathbf{R}.

It follows that the identity (
48
) holds.
Note that the operator
\mathbf{R}
is self-adjoint, since

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{R}u,\widetilde{u}\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\int^{t_{1}}_{t_{0}}{\widetilde{u}(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}{B(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}{\Phi(t_{1},t)}^{\mathrm{H}}Q(t_{1})\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}u(t_{1})\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle={\left(\int^{t_{1}}_{t_{0}}\Phi(t_{1},t)B(t)\widetilde{u}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\right)}^{\mathrm{H}}Q(t_{1})\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}u(t_{1})={\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}\widetilde{u}(t_{1})}^{\mathrm{H}}Q(t_{1})\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}u(t_{1}),

and therefore

\langle u,\mathbf{R}\widetilde{u}\rangle_{L^{2}}=\overline{\langle\mathbf{R}\widetilde{u},u\rangle_{L^{2}}}=\overline{{\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}u(t_{1})}^{\mathrm{H}}Q(t_{1})\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}\widetilde{u}(t_{1})}={\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}\widetilde{u}(t_{1})}^{\mathrm{H}}Q(t_{1})\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}u(t_{1})=\langle\mathbf{R}u,\widetilde{u}\rangle_{L^{2}},

for all
u,\widetilde{u}\in L^{2}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{m})
, i.e.,
\mathbf{R}={\mathbf{R}}^{\mathrm{H}}
.
Finally, if
Q\geq 0
, then in particular

\langle\mathbf{R}u,u\rangle_{L^{2}}={\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}u(t_{1})}^{\mathrm{H}}Q(t_{1})\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}u(t_{1})=\lVert\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}u(t_{1})\rVert_{Q(t_{1})}\geq 0

holds for all
u\in L^{2}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{m})
, i.e.,
\mathbf{R}\geq 0
.
∎

By
Theorem

3.5
, we already know that
(KYP)
implies
(NN)
. However,
Lemma

6.13
provides an explicit relation between the
(KYP)
and the Popov operator
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t_{0},t_{1}}
and we can directly obtain the following result.

Corollary 6.14
.

Suppose that there exists
Q\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
that satisfies the KYP inequality (
4
). Then the system has a nonnegative supply
(NN)
.

Proof.

Because of
Lemma

6.13
, we have

\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t_{0},t_{1}}={\begin{bmatrix}\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}\\
\mathrm{Id}\end{bmatrix}}^{\mathrm{H}}\begin{bmatrix}-{A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q-Q{A}^{\mathrm{H}}-\dot{Q}&{C}^{\mathrm{H}}-QB\\
C-{B}^{\mathrm{H}}Q&D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\Psi_{t_{0},t_{1}}\\
\mathrm{Id}\end{bmatrix}+\mathbf{R}\geq 0.\qed

By
Corollary

6.6
, if
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t_{0},t_{1}}
is positive semidefinite, the system fulfills
(NN)
.

Remark 6.15
.

In this section, it was shown that complete reachability is a sufficient condition to obtain a
(pH)
realization from a given LTV system (
1
) with
(NN)
supply and
D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}}\geq cI_{m}
. Thus, in order to obtain a
(pH)
realization, one has to extract a completely reachable realization using the global Kalman canonical decomposition
[
28
]
. If the extracted realization (using the same approach) is also completely reconstructable, then
Q
in
(KYP)
and
(pH)
is also invertible.

7
Applications

This section considers examples of physical systems that naturally lead to LTV systems. Furthermore, it is shown that these systems can be formulated as time-varying
(pH)
systems, and therefore they are also passive
(Pa)
and have nonnegative supply
(NN)
.

7.1
Motion of time-varying masses

Motivated by the so-called “rocket problems”, where the mass of objects in motion decreases with time due to fuel consumption, we revisit an example from
[
44
]
.

The equations of motion of the time-varying mass can be described by

\displaystyle\dot{v}m+v_{e}\dot{m}=F_{\mathrm{ext}}

(49)

where
m
and
v
are the total mass and velocity of the rocket,

v_{e}
is the effective exhaust velocity of the fuel (often assumed to be constant), and
F_{\mathrm{ext}}
combines the external forces, e.g. gravitational force or drag force.

Assume that the rocket movement is one-dimensional with
z
denoting the height, then
v=\dot{z}
and we consider the momentum
p=m\dot{z}
whose evolution is described by

\dot{p}=\dot{m}v+m\dot{v}=\dot{m}v-v_{e}\dot{m}+F_{\mathrm{ext}}=\tfrac{\dot{m}}{m}p-v_{e}\dot{m}+F_{\mathrm{ext}},

where we used (
49
).
If we assume that the total mass of the rocket
m
is controlled independently, weakly decreasing in time but always positive, and by using the trivial equation
\dot{z}=\tfrac{p}{m}
, we obtain the following LTV system.

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\dot{z}(t)\\
\dot{p}(t)\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}0&\tfrac{1}{m(t)}\\
0&\tfrac{\dot{m}(t)}{m(t)}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}z(t)\\
p(t)\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
-\dot{m}(t)&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}v_{e}(t)\\
F_{\mathrm{ext}}(t)\end{bmatrix}.

Letting the Hamiltonian represent the kinetic energy leads to

\mathcal{H}(t,z,p)=\frac{p^{2}}{2m(t)}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}z\\
p\end{bmatrix}^{\top}\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
0&\tfrac{1}{m(t)}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}z\\
p\end{bmatrix},\quad Q(t):=\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
0&\tfrac{1}{m(t)}\end{bmatrix}.

Using the null space decomposition and
Theorem

3.8
, we obtain

K(t)=\frac{1}{2}Q^{-1}(t)\dot{Q}(t)=\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
0&-\tfrac{\dot{m}(t)}{m(t)}\end{bmatrix},

which leads to the pH formulation

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{bmatrix}\dot{z}(t)\\
\dot{p}(t)\end{bmatrix}&=\Bigg(\Bigg(\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{bmatrix}}_{=:J(t)}-\underbrace{\tfrac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
0&-\dot{m}(t)\end{bmatrix}}_{=:R(t)}\Bigg)Q(t)-K(t)\Bigg)\begin{bmatrix}z(t)\\
p(t)\end{bmatrix}+\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
-\dot{m}(t)&1\end{bmatrix}}_{=:G(t)}\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}v_{e}(t)\\
F_{\mathrm{ext}}(t)\end{bmatrix}}_{=:u(t)},\\
y(t)&=G(t)^{\top}Q(t)\begin{bmatrix}z(t)\\
p(t)\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}0&-\tfrac{\dot{m}(t)}{m(t)}\\
0&\tfrac{1}{m(t)}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}z(t)\\
p(t)\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}-\dot{m}(t)v(t)\\
v(t)\end{bmatrix},\end{split}

where
P
,
S
, and
N
in (
1.1
) are set to zero and the output
y
is defined in a collocated way.
In order to integrate gravitational effects into the model, the external force can be substituted by
F_{\mathrm{ext}}=-mg
