. Alternatively, this force could be incorporated into the Hamiltonian by including an additional term
mgz
. However, this modification makes the Hamiltonian a quadratic polynomial unbounded from below, instead of a positive semidefinite quadratic form, rendering the system non-port-Hamiltonian in the classical linear framework. Despite this, such Hamiltonians can be analyzed within the framework of cyclo-passive systems
[
26
]
.

7.2
District heating networks with stratified storage models

Linear time-varying pH systems appear in the context of district heating systems that contain more detailed (stratified) storage tank models
[
36
,
50
]
. In this model, water storage is subdivided into a hot and a cold layer having time-varying volumes
V_{h}
and
V_{c}
and it is assumed that within each of these volumes, the temperature is homogeneous and given by
T_{h}
and
T_{c}
, respectively. Furthermore, mass flows
q_{p}(t)>0
and
q_{d}(t)>0
are given for all
t\in\mathbb{T}
on the production and demand side that satisfy
q_{p},q_{d}\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}([0,\infty),\mathbb{R})
.
The operation of the system can be described in the following way: Heat is supplied from the hot layer through a mass flow
q_{d}
with temperature
T_{h}
and there is a return mass flow
q_{d}
with temperature
T_{\textrm{in},d}
into the cold layer. Furthermore, there is a mass flow
q_{p}
leaving the cold layer with temperature
T_{c}
that is heated by a producer, resulting in a temperature
T_{\textrm{out},p}
that enters the hot layer of the tank. In summary, we obtain the following set of equations

\displaystyle\tfrac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}(V_{h}(t)T_{h}(t))

\displaystyle=q_{p}(t)T_{\textrm{in},p}(t)-q_{d}(t)T_{h}(t),

(50)

\displaystyle\tfrac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}(V_{c}(t)T_{c}(t))

\displaystyle=q_{d}(t)T_{\textrm{in},d}(t)-q_{p}(t)T_{c}(t),

(51)

\displaystyle\tfrac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}V_{h}(t)

\displaystyle=q_{p}(t)-q_{d}(t).

(52)

Since
q_{p}
and
q_{d}
are fixed, the volume of the hot layer is given by

V_{h}(t)=V_{h,0}+\int_{0}^{t}\big(q_{d}(\tau)-q_{s}(\tau)\big)\mathrm{d}\tau,\quad V_{c}(t)=V_{s}-V_{h}(t),\quad\mbox{\rm for all }t\in\mathbb{T},

where
V_{s}
is the total mass of the storage, and we assume that
V_{h}
and
V_{c}
are uniformly positive. Hence, we can neglect (
52
) and the remaining system (
50
) and (
51
) is of the form (
1
) with

A(t)=\begin{bmatrix}-\frac{q_{d}(t)}{V_{h}(t)}&0\\
0&-\frac{q_{p}(t)}{V_{c}(t)}\end{bmatrix},\quad B(t)=\begin{bmatrix}q_{p}(t)&0\\
0&q_{d}(t)\end{bmatrix},\quad x=\begin{bmatrix}V_{h}T_{h}\\
V_{c}T_{c}\end{bmatrix},\quad u=\begin{bmatrix}T_{\textrm{in},p}\\
T_{\textrm{in},d}\end{bmatrix}.

The energy of the system is described by the following Hamiltonian

\mathcal{H}(t,T_{c},T_{h})=\tfrac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}T_{h}\\
T_{c}\end{bmatrix}^{\top}Q(t)\begin{bmatrix}T_{h}\\
T_{c}\end{bmatrix},\quad Q(t):=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{1}{V_{h}(t)}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{V_{c}(t)}\end{bmatrix}.

Furthermore,
Q
solves the KYP inequality

\displaystyle-{A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q-Q{A}^{\mathrm{H}}-\dot{Q}

\displaystyle=2\begin{bmatrix}\frac{q_{d}}{V_{h}^{2}}&0\\
0&\frac{q_{p}}{V_{c}^{2}}\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\dot{V}_{h}}{V_{h}^{2}}&0\\
0&\frac{\dot{V}_{c}}{V_{c}^{2}}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=2\begin{bmatrix}\frac{q_{d}}{V_{h}^{2}}&0\\
0&\frac{q_{p}}{V_{c}^{2}}\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}\frac{q_{p}-q_{d}}{V_{h}^{2}}&0\\
0&\frac{q_{d}-q_{p}}{V_{c}^{2}}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{q_{d}+q_{c}}{V_{h}^{2}}&0\\
&\frac{q_{d}+q_{c}}{V_{c}^{2}}\end{bmatrix}\geq 0.

Since
Q
is pointwise invertible, the LTV pH system is given by setting

K(t)=\tfrac{1}{2}Q^{-1}(t)\dot{Q}(t)=-\tfrac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}\frac{q_{p}(t)-q_{d}(t)}{V_{h}(t)}&0\\
0&\frac{q_{d}(t)-q_{p}(t)}{V_{c}(t)}\end{bmatrix}.

Then the pH system representation (
1.1
) is given by

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\dot{x}_{1}\\
\dot{x}_{2}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\Bigg(-\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}q_{d}+q_{p}&0\\
0&q_{d}+q_{p}\end{bmatrix}}_{=:R(t)}\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}\frac{1}{V_{h}}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{V_{c}}\end{bmatrix}}_{=Q}-K\Bigg)\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}\\
x_{2}\end{bmatrix}+\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}q_{p}&0\\
0&q_{d}\end{bmatrix}}_{=G}\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}T_{\textrm{in},p}\\
T_{\textrm{in},d}\end{bmatrix}}_{=u}

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle={G}^{\mathrm{H}}Qx=\begin{bmatrix}q_{p}T_{h}\\
q_{d}T_{c}\end{bmatrix},\quad\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}\\
x_{2}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}V_{h}T_{h}\\
V_{c}T_{c}\end{bmatrix},

where the matrices
P
,
J
,
S
, and
N
in (
1.1
) are set to zero and the output
y
is defined in the collocated way.

8
Conclusion

This paper presents a modified definition of linear time-varying (LTV) port-Hamiltonian systems
(pH)
and studies the relation to passivity
(Pa)
, the existence of solutions of the Kalman-Yakubovich-Popov inequality
(KYP)
and the available storage to be finite. In addition, the positive real property for time-invariant systems is generalized to time-varying systems by studying the nonnegativity property
(NN)
. The necessary and sufficient conditions obtained between all these properties are summarized in Figure
2
.

(pH)
(KYP)
(Pa) and
Q\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n,n})
(Pa)
finite
available storage and
Q\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n,n})
finite
available storage
(NN)
Thm.

Thm.

Figure 2:
The relationship between
(pH)
,
(KYP)
,
(Pa)
and
(NN)
for linear time-varying system (
1
). The blue color marks implications without additional assumptions and the black color sufficient implications with additional assumptions. Counterexamples can be found in
[
13
]
.

Compared to the linear-time invariant (LTI) case, the relation between
(Pa)
and
(KYP)
is more involved because
(Pa)
does not guarantee the existence of a weakly differentiable solution to
(KYP)
.
Finally, we presented some examples of systems with time-varying masses that can be formulated as LTV pH systems. The presented linear time-varying pH framework can also be used for structure-preserving local linearization of nonlinear pH systems into linear time-varying pH systems or to formulate switched pH systems. Future work will include the extension to descriptor systems as well as scattering passive systems.

Appendix A
Technical lemmas

In the paper, we often refer to the following generalization of the Hölder inequality.

Theorem A.1

(Generalized Hölder inequality)
.

Let
F\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{l,m})
and
G\in L^{q}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m,n})
, where
1\leq p,q,r\leq\infty
and
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{r}
. Then
FG\in L^{r}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{l,n})
with

\lVert FG\rVert_{L^{r}}\leq\lVert F\rVert_{L^{p}}\lVert G\rVert_{L^{q}}
.

Proof.

By definition we have

\lVert FG\rVert_{L^{r}}=\big\lVert\lVert FG\rVert_{2}\big\rVert_{L^{r}}\leq\big\lVert\lVert F\rVert_{2}\lVert G\rVert_{2}\big\rVert_{L^{r}}\leq\big\lVert\lVert F\rVert_{2}\big\rVert_{L^{p}}\big\lVert\lVert G\rVert_{2}\big\rVert_{L^{q}}=\lVert F\rVert_{L^{p}}\lVert G\rVert_{L^{q}},

where we applied the better known scalar generalized Hölder inequality to
\lVert F\rVert_{2}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})
and
\lVert G\rVert_{2}\in L^{q}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})
, see e.g.
[
8
, Remark 2 in Chapter 4]

∎

Lemma A.2
.

Let
A\in L^{1}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{n,m})
. Then for every
t_{0}\in\mathbb{T}
the map

\mathbf{A}_{t_{0}}:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{C},\qquad t\mapsto\int_{t_{0}}^{t}A(s)\,\mathrm{d}s

satisfies
\mathbf{A}_{t_{0}}\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n,m})
and
\dot{\mathbf{A}}_{t_{0}}=A
.

Proof.

Let
A=[a_{ij}]\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}^{2n,2m})
. Then we can equivalently prove the statement for every entry
a_{ij}\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})
.
Let
K\subseteq\mathbb{T}
be any compact subset and let
[a,b]\subseteq\mathbb{T}
be any compact subinterval such that
K\cup\{t_{0}\}\subseteq[a,b]
.
Then, it is sufficient to show that, given
f\in L^{1}([a,b],\mathbb{R})
, the function
F:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R},\ t\mapsto\int_{t_{0}}^{t}f(s)\,\mathrm{d}s
satisfies
F\in W^{1,1}([a,b],\mathbb{R})
and
\dot{F}=f
.
Due to
[
11
, Theorem 20.9 and Lemma 20.14]
, we see that the map
\widetilde{F}:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R},\ t\mapsto\int_{a}^{t}f(s)\,\mathrm{d}s
satisfies
\widetilde{F}\in W^{1,1}([a,b],\mathbb{R})
and
\dot{\widetilde{F}}=f
. Since

F(t)=\int_{a}^{t}f(s)\,\mathrm{d}s=\int_{t_{0}}^{t}f(s)\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{a}^{t_{0}}f(s)\,\mathrm{d}s=\widetilde{F}(t)+c

for all
t\in[a,b]
, with a constant
c\in\mathbb{R}
depending only on
a,t_{0},f
, we deduce that also
F\in W^{1,1}([a,b],\mathbb{R})
and
\dot{F}=\dot{\widetilde{F}}=f
.
∎

Corollary A.3
.

Let
A\in L^{p}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{n,m})
with
1\leq p<\infty
. Then for every
\varepsilon>0
there is
\delta>0
such that

\int_{r}^{s}\lVert A(t)\rVert_{2}\,\mathrm{d}t<(s-r)^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\varepsilon

holds for every
r,s\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
with
0<s-r<\delta
.

Proof.

Let
a:[t_{0},t_{1}]\to\mathbb{R},\ t\mapsto\lVert A(t)\rVert_{2}^{p}
. Then, in particular,
a\in L^{1}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{R})
. Let

f:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R},\qquad t\mapsto\int_{t_{0}}^{t}a(s)\,\mathrm{d}s,

which is in
W^{1,1}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{R})
, because of
Lemma

A.2
.
In particular,
f
is uniformly continuous, so there exists
\delta>0
such that

\int_{r}^{s}\lVert A(t)\rVert^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{r}^{s}a(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=f(s)-f(r)<\varepsilon^{p}

By applying the generalized Hölder inequality (
Theorem

A.1
), we obtain

\int_{r}^{s}\lVert A(t)\rVert\,\mathrm{d}\leq\left(\int_{r}^{s}1\,\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\left(\int_{r}^{s}\lVert A(t)\rVert^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}<(s-r)^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\varepsilon.\qed

Lemma A.4
.

Let
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})
with
1\leq p\leq\infty
and suppose that for every
t\in\mathbb{T}
we are given a subset
\mathbb{T}_{t}\subseteq\mathbb{T}
such that the quantities

a\coloneqq\sup_{t\in\mathbb{T},\,s\in\mathbb{T}_{t}}\lvert t-s\rvert\qquad\text{and}\qquad b\coloneqq\inf_{t\in\mathbb{T}}\lvert\mathbb{T}_{t}\rvert

are bounded and positive. Suppose additionally that the map
\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R},\ t\mapsto\lvert\mathbb{T}_{t}\rvert
is measurable.
Then the map

\widetilde{f}:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R},\qquad t\mapsto\frac{1}{\lvert\mathbb{T}_{t}\rvert}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(s)\,\mathrm{d}s

is in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
, satisfies
\lVert\widetilde{f}\rVert_{L^{p}}\leq(\frac{2a}{b})^{1/p}\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}}
, and can be written equivalently as

\widetilde{f}(t)=(\varphi_{t}\star\overline{f})(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varphi_{t}(t-s)\overline{f}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s,

(53)

where
\star
denotes the convolution product. Here, the map
\varphi_{t}
is defined as

\varphi_{t}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},\qquad s\mapsto\frac{\chi_{\mathbb{T}_{t}}(t-s)}{\lvert\mathbb{T}_{t}\rvert}

for all
t\in\mathbb{T}
, and
\overline{f}
denotes the extension of
f
to
L^{p}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
, obtained by setting
\overline{f}(s)=0
for
s\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{T}
.

Proof.

This statement and its proof closely follow Young’s theorem (see, e.g.
[
8
, Theorem 4.15]
), which cannot be applied directly, since
\varphi_{t}
is parameterized with
t
.

By construction, for every
t\in\mathbb{T}
it holds that
\varphi_{t}\in L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}
with
\lVert\varphi_{t}\rVert_{L^{1}}=\lVert\varphi_{t}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}=1
.
Furthermore, we have that

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varphi_{t}(t-s)\overline{f}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\chi_{\mathbb{T}_{t}}(s)}{\lvert\mathbb{T}_{t}\rvert}\overline{f}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathbb{T}_{s}\rvert}\int_{\mathbb{T}_{t}}\overline{f}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s

for every
t\in\mathbb{T}
, thus (
53
) holds.

It remains to show that
\widetilde{f}\in L^{p}
with the requested bound on its norm.
Suppose first that
p=\infty
. In this case, it clearly holds that

\lvert g(t)\rvert\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\lvert\varphi_{t}(t-s)\rvert\lVert\overline{f}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\,\mathrm{d}s=\lVert f\rVert_{L^{\infty}}

for all
t\in\mathbb{T}
, in particular
g\in L^{\infty}
and
\lVert g\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq\lVert f\rVert_{L^{\infty}}
, as requested.

Suppose now that
1\leq p<\infty
. For a.e.
s\in\mathbb{T}
it holds that

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\lvert\varphi_{t}(t-s)\rvert\lvert\overline{f}(s)\rvert^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\lvert\overline{f}(s)\rvert^{p}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\chi_{\mathbb{T}_{t}}(s)}{\lvert\mathbb{T}_{t}\rvert}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\lvert\overline{f}(s)\rvert^{p}\int_{s-a}^{s+a}\frac{1}{\lvert\mathbb{T}_{t}\rvert}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\frac{2a}{b}\lvert\overline{f}(s)\rvert^{p}<\infty.

In particular, we deduce from Fubini’s theorem
[
8
, Theorem 4.5]
that

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\lvert\varphi_{t}(t-s)\rvert\lvert\overline{f}(s)\rvert^{p}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\lvert\varphi_{t}(t-s)\rvert\lvert\overline{f}(s)\rvert^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}s\leq\frac{2a}{b}\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}}^{p}<\infty.

Then, by splitting
\varphi_{t}(t-s)\lvert\overline{f}(s)\rvert=\varphi_{t}(t-s)^{1/p^{\prime}}\varphi_{t}(t-s)^{1/p}\lvert\overline{f}(s)\rvert
with
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=1
and applying Hölder’s inequality, we obtain that

\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varphi_{t}(t-s)\lvert\overline{f}(s)\rvert\,\mathrm{d}s\right\rvert^{p}\leq\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varphi_{t}(t-s)\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{\frac{p}{p^{\prime}}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\lvert\varphi_{t}(t-s)\rvert\lvert\overline{f}(s)\rvert^{p}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\lvert\varphi_{t}(t-s)\rvert\lvert\overline{f}(s)\rvert^{p}\,\mathrm{d}s,

and therefore

\lVert g\rVert_{L^{p}}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varphi_{t}(t-s)\lvert\overline{f}(s)\rvert\,\mathrm{d}s\right\rvert^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\lvert\varphi_{t}(t-s)\rvert\lvert\overline{f}(s)\rvert^{p}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq\left(\frac{2a}{b}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}},

as asserted.
∎

Theorem A.5
.

Let
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})
with
1\leq p<\infty
and suppose that for every
t\in\mathbb{T}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
we are given a subset
\mathbb{T}_{t,k}\subseteq\mathbb{T}
such that the quantities

a_{k}\coloneqq\sup_{t\in\mathbb{T},\,s\in\mathbb{T}_{t,k}}\lvert t-s\rvert\qquad\text{and}\qquad b_{k}\coloneqq\inf_{t\in\mathbb{T}}\lvert\mathbb{T}_{t,k}\rvert

are bounded and satisfy
\lim_{k\to\infty}a_{k}=0
and
b_{k}\geq ca_{k}>0
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, where
c>0
is a fixed constant.
Suppose additionally that the map
\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R},\ t\mapsto\lvert\mathbb{T}_{t,k}\rvert
is measurable for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then the sequence of functions
\{f_{k}:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}\mid k\in\mathbb{N}\}
defined as

\widetilde{f}_{k}(t)\coloneqq\frac{1}{\lvert\mathbb{T}_{t,k}\rvert}\int_{\mathbb{T}_{t,k}}f(s)\,\mathrm{d}s

satisfies
\widetilde{f}_{k}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and converges to
f
in
L^{p}
for
k\to\infty
.

Proof.

Because of
Lemma

A.4
, we can write equivalently
\widetilde{f}_{k}(t)=(\varphi_{t,k}\star\overline{f})(t)
with

\varphi_{t,k}(t)=\frac{\chi_{\mathbb{T}_{t,k}}(t-s)}{\lvert\mathbb{T}_{t,k}\rvert^{-1}},

and
\widetilde{f}_{k}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})
holds for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Since the continuous functions with compact support are dense in
L^{p}
, for every
\varepsilon>0
there exists
f_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{C}_{c}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
such that
\lVert f_{\varepsilon}-\overline{f}\rVert_{L^{p}}<\varepsilon
. Let us define now
\widetilde{f}_{\varepsilon,k}:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R},\ t\mapsto(\varphi_{t,k}\star f_{\varepsilon})(t)
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Since
\varphi_{t,k}(t-s)f_{\varepsilon}(s)=0
for every
s\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{T}
, we have equivalently
\widetilde{f}_{\varepsilon,k}(t)=(\varphi_{t,k}\star\overline{f_{\varepsilon}|_{\mathbb{T}}})(t)
, thus we can apply to this function the results of
Lemma

A.4
.
Clearly

\lVert\widetilde{f}_{k}-f\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T})}\leq\lVert\widetilde{f}_{k}-\widetilde{f}_{\varepsilon,k}\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T})}+\lVert\widetilde{f}_{\varepsilon,k}-f_{\varepsilon}|_{\mathbb{T}}\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T})}+\lVert f_{\varepsilon}|_{\mathbb{T}}-f\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T})}

holds for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
For every
t\in\mathbb{T}
it holds that

\widetilde{f}_{k}(t)-\widetilde{f}_{\varepsilon,k}(t)=(\varphi_{t,k}\star\overline{f})(t)-(\varphi_{t,k}\star\overline{f_{\varepsilon}|_{\mathbb{T}}})(t)=\big(\varphi_{t,k}\star\overline{(f-f_{\varepsilon}|_{\mathbb{T}})}\big)(t),

in particular,

\lVert\widetilde{f}_{k}-\widetilde{f}_{\varepsilon,k}\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T})}\leq\left(\frac{2a_{k}}{b_{k}}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\lVert f-f_{\varepsilon}|_{\mathbb{T}}\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T})}\leq c^{\frac{1}{p}}\varepsilon.

Since
f_{\varepsilon}
is continuous with compact support, it is in particular uniformly continuous, thus for every
\varepsilon_{1}>0
there exists
\delta>0
such that
\lvert f_{\varepsilon}(s)-f_{\varepsilon}(t)\rvert<\varepsilon_{1}
for every
s,t\in\mathbb{T}
with
\lvert t-s\rvert<\delta
.
Since
\lim_{k\to\infty}a_{k}=0
, there exists
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
a_{k}<\delta
whenever
k\geq k_{0}
. In particular, for every
t\in\mathbb{T}
and
s\in\mathbb{T}_{t,k}
with
k\geq k_{0}
it holds that
\lvert t-s\rvert<\delta
.
Furthermore, if we denote by
K_{\varepsilon}
the support of
f_{\varepsilon}
, by
B_{1}\coloneqq\{s\in\mathbb{R},\ \lvert s\rvert<1\}
the unit ball, and we assume without loss of generality that
\delta<1
, it follows that
f_{\varepsilon}(s)=f_{\varepsilon}(t)=0
for all
s\in\mathbb{T}_{t,k}
whenever
t\in\mathbb{T}\setminus(K_{\varepsilon}+B_{1})
and
k\geq k_{0}
.
Note that the set
\mathbb{T}_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq\mathbb{T}\cap(K_{\varepsilon}+B_{1})
has finite measure and does not depend on the choice of
\varepsilon_{1}
.
Therefore, by exploiting the fact that
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varphi_{t,k}(t-s)\,\mathrm{d}s=1
for every fixed
t\in\mathbb{T}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left\lvert\widetilde{f}_{\varepsilon,k}(t)-f_{\varepsilon}|_{\mathbb{T}}(t)\right\rvert^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varphi_{t,k}(t-s)f_{\varepsilon}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varphi_{t,k}(t-s)f_{\varepsilon}(t)\,\mathrm{d}s\right\rvert^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varphi_{t,k}(t-s)\lvert f_{\varepsilon}(s)-f_{\varepsilon}(t)\rvert\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left(\frac{1}{\lvert\mathbb{T}_{t,k}\rvert}\int_{\mathbb{T}_{t,k}}\lvert f_{\varepsilon}(s)-f_{\varepsilon}(t)\rvert\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\varepsilon}}\left(\frac{1}{\lvert\mathbb{T}_{t,k}\rvert}\lvert\mathbb{T}_{t,k}\rvert\varepsilon_{1}\right)^{p}=\varepsilon_{1}^{p}\lvert\mathbb{T}_{\varepsilon}\rvert,

and thus
\lVert\widetilde{f}_{\varepsilon,k}-f_{\varepsilon}|_{\mathbb{T}}\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T})}\leq\varepsilon_{1}\lvert\mathbb{T}_{\varepsilon}\rvert^{1/p}
, for every
k\geq k_{0}
. We deduce that

\lVert\widetilde{f}_{k}-f\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T})}\leq\big(c^{\frac{1}{p}}+1\big)\varepsilon+\varepsilon_{1}\lvert\mathbb{T}_{\varepsilon}\rvert^{\frac{1}{p}}.

It is then clear, by taking
\varepsilon
arbitrarily small and e.g.
\varepsilon_{1}<\varepsilon\lvert\mathbb{T}_{\varepsilon}\rvert^{-\frac{1}{p}}
, that
\widetilde{f}_{k}
converges to
f
in
L^{p}
.
∎

Lemma A.6
.

For all
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathbb{T},\ t_{0}\leq t_{1}
the Popov operator
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t_{0},t_{1}}
as in (
39
) is a well-defined bounded linear operator.

Proof.

Let us split
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t_{0},t_{1}}=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{2}+\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{3}
such that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{1}u(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{t_{0}}^{t}C(t)\Phi(t,s)B(s)u(s)\,\mathrm{d}s,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{2}u(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{t}^{t_{1}}{B(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}{\Phi(s,t)}^{\mathrm{H}}{C(s)}^{\mathrm{H}}u(s)\,\mathrm{d}s,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{3}u(t)

\displaystyle=\big(D(t)+{D(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}\big)u(t).

We will show first that
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{1}
,
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{2}
and
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{3}
are well defined, linear, and bounded, so that
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t_{0},t_{1}}
inherits the same properties.
It is clear that, if they are well defined, then they are linear.
For every
u\in L^{2}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{m})
it holds that

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\left\lVert\int_{t_{0}}^{t}C(t)\Phi(t,s)B(s)u(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\right\rVert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t=

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}{u(s)}^{\mathrm{H}}{B(s)}^{\mathrm{H}}{\Phi(t,s)}^{\mathrm{H}}{C(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}C(t)\Phi(t,r)B(r)u(r)\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}t\leq

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\lVert C(t)\rVert^{2}\lVert\Phi(t,r)\rVert\lVert\Phi(t,s)\rVert\lVert B(r)\rVert\lVert B(s)\rVert\lVert u(r)\rVert\lVert u(s)\rVert\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}t\leq

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\lVert\Phi\rVert_{L^{\infty}([t_{0},t_{1}])}^{2}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\lVert C(t)\rVert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\left(\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\lVert B(s)\rVert\lVert u(s)\rVert\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\lVert\Phi\rVert_{L^{\infty}([t_{0},t_{1}])}^{2}\lVert C\rVert_{L^{2}([t_{0},t_{1}])}^{2}\lVert B\rVert_{L^{2}([t_{0},t_{1}])}^{2}\lVert u\rVert_{L^{2}([t_{0},t_{1}])}^{2},

thus
\lVert\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{1}u\rVert_{L^{2}}\leq\lVert\Phi\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert C\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert B\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert u\rVert_{L^{2}}
and therefore
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{1}
is well-defined and bounded. The proof for
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{2}
is analogous, while for
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{3}
it immediately follows from

\lVert(D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}})u\rVert_{L^{2}}\leq 2\lVert D\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert u\rVert_{L^{\infty}}.

∎

Lemma A.7
.

.
For every
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathbb{T},\ t_{0}\leq t_{1}
the local transfer operator
\mathbf{Z}_{t_{0},t_{1}}
as in (
42
) is bounded.

Proof.

Since
\Phi\in L^{\infty}
, we obtain for every
u\in L^{2}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{m})
that

\displaystyle\lVert\mathbf{Z}_{t_{0},t_{1}}u\rVert_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\left\lVert\int_{t_{0}}^{t}C(t)\Phi(t,s)B(s)u(s)\,\mathrm{d}s+D(t)u(t)\right\rVert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\tfrac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\Phi\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\left(\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\left(\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\lVert C(t)\rVert\lVert B(s)\rVert\lVert u(s)\rVert\,\mathrm{d}s+\lVert D\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert u(t)\rVert\right)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\tfrac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\Phi\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\left(\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\lVert C(t)\rVert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\left(\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\lVert B(s)\rVert\lVert u(s)\rVert\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{2}+\lVert D\rVert_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\lVert u(t)\rVert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\tfrac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\Phi\rVert_{L^{\infty}}(\lVert C\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\lVert B\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\lVert u\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\lVert D\rVert_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\lVert u\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2})^{\tfrac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\Phi\rVert_{L^{\infty}}(\lVert B\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert C\rVert_{L^{2}}+\lVert D\rVert_{L^{\infty}})\lVert u\rVert_{L^{2}},

in particular
\lVert\mathbf{Z}_{t_{0},t_{1}}\rVert\leq\lVert\Phi\rVert_{L^{\infty}}(\lVert B\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert C\rVert_{L^{2}}+\lVert D\rVert_{L^{\infty}})<\infty
, and hence
\mathbf{Z}_{t_{0},t_{1}}
is bounded.
∎

Lemma A.8
.

Let
f\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})
such that
\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}f(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\geq 0
holds for all
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathbb{T},\ t_{0}\leq t_{1}
. Then
f(t)\geq 0
for a.e.
t\in\mathbb{T}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbb{T}_{N}\subseteq\mathbb{T}
for
N\in\mathbb{N}
be any sequence of compact intervals such that
\mathbb{T}_{N}\subseteq\mathbb{T}_{N+1}
for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbb{T}=\bigcup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{T}_{N}
, let
E\coloneqq\{t\in\mathbb{T}\mid f(t)<0\}
and let
E_{N}\coloneqq E\cap\mathbb{T}_{N}
for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
.

Fix any
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Since
E_{N}
is measurable, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists an open set
\Omega_{n}\subseteq\mathbb{T}
such that
E_{N}\subseteq\Omega_{n}
and
\lvert\Omega_{n}\setminus E_{N}\rvert<\frac{1}{n}
. We assume without loss of generality that
\Omega_{n+1}\subseteq\Omega_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, up to replacing
\Omega_{n+1}
with
\Omega_{n}\cap\Omega_{n+1}
.
For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
the set
\Omega_{n}
is open, and therefore there are at most countably many disjoint intervals
I_{n,k}
such that
\Omega_{n}=\bigcup_{k}I_{n,k}
. In particular, we have that

\int_{\Omega_{n}}f(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=\sum_{k}\int_{I_{n,k}}f(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\geq 0

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. It follows that

\int_{E_{N}}f(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{\Omega_{n}}f(t)\,\mathrm{d}t-\int_{\Omega_{n}\setminus E_{N}}f(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\geq-\int_{\Omega_{n}\setminus E_{N}}f(t)\,\mathrm{d}t,

and, therefore,

\int_{E_{N}}f(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\geq-\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Omega_{n}\setminus E_{N}}f(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=0,

because of the dominated convergence theorem, see e.g.
[
8
, Theorem 4.2]
. Since
f<0
on
E_{N}
, we conclude that necessarily
\lvert E_{N}\rvert=0
.

Thus, since
E=\bigcup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}E_{N}
, we deduce that

\lvert E\rvert=\left\lvert\bigcup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}E_{N}\right\rvert\leq\sum_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\lvert E_{N}\rvert=0,

thus
f\geq 0
a.e. on
\mathbb{T}
.
∎

Lemma A.9
.

Let
A\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n,m})
such that
\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}A(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=0
for all
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathbb{T},\ t_{0}\leq t_{1}
. Then
A(t)=0
for a.e.
t\in\mathbb{T}
.

Proof.

If
A=[a_{ij}]\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}^{2n,2m})
, then
a_{ij}\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})
satisfies
\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}a_{ij}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=0
for all
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathbb{T},\ t_{0}\leq t_{1}
and all
i,j
. In particular, applying
Lemma

A.8
to
a_{ij}
and
-a_{ij}
, we obtain
a_{ij}(t)=0
for a.e.
t\in\mathbb{T}
, for all
i,j
.
We conclude that also
A(t)=0
for a.e.
t\in\mathbb{T}
.
∎

Lemma A.10
.

Let
Q\in W^{1,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{++}(\mathbb{C}))
for some
p\in[1,\infty]
.
Then there exists a lower triangular matrix function
L\in W^{1,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
with real and positive diagonal entries such that
Q(t)={L(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}L(t)
for all
t\in\mathbb{T}
.

Proof.

We prove the statement by induction on the dimension of
Q
, exploiting the explicit construction of the Cholesky decomposition.
Write

Q=\begin{bmatrix}\alpha&b\\
{b}^{\mathrm{H}}&Q_{1}\end{bmatrix}

with
\alpha\in W^{1,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}_{+})
,
b\in W^{1,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n-1})
and
Q_{1}\in W^{1,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n-1}_{++}(\mathbb{C}))
.
The pointwise Cholesky decomposition of
Q
is then given by
{L}^{\mathrm{H}}L
with

L=\begin{bmatrix}\alpha^{\tfrac{1}{2}}&\alpha^{-\tfrac{1}{2}}b\\
0&L_{1}\end{bmatrix},

where
L_{1}
is constructed recursively as the Cholesky factor of
Q_{1}+\alpha^{-1}{b}^{\mathrm{H}}b
.
Let us denote by
f,g\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R}_{+})
the functions

f:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+},\ s\mapsto s^{\tfrac{1}{2}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad g:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+},\ s\mapsto s^{-1}.

Then, the functions
\alpha^{\tfrac{1}{2}}=f\circ\alpha
and
\alpha^{-\tfrac{1}{2}}=g\circ f\circ\alpha
are in
W^{1,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}
, and so is
\alpha^{-\tfrac{1}{2}}b
.
If
n=1
, then only
\alpha^{\tfrac{1}{2}}
appears and we conclude that
L
is in
W^{1,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}
.
Otherwise,
L_{1}
is also in
W^{1,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}
by the induction hypothesis, so we have
L
is in
W^{1,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}
.
∎

Appendix B
Solution theory

In this section, we recall conditions for the existence and uniqueness of solutions of (linear) time-varying equations, and the properties of the associated fundamental solution and state-transition matrices.
We start by recalling that the conditions that we assumed for the coefficient matrices in (
1
) guarantee the existence of global solutions and their uniqueness up to fixing one point.

Theorem B.1
.

Let
A\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n,n})
and
b\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n})
. Then for every
(t_{0},x_{0})\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}
the ordinary differential equation

\dot{x}=A(t)x+b(t)

(54)

has exactly one solution
x\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n})
such that
x(t_{0})=x_{0}
.

Proof.

The proof is exactly that of
[
21
, Theorem 3]
, up to identifying
\mathbb{C}
with
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
∎

Corollary B.2
.

For every initial condition
(t_{0},x_{0})\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}
and input
u\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m})
, the LTV system (
1
) has exactly one solution
x\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n})
that satisfies
x(t_{0})=x_{0}
. Furthermore, the corresponding output
y
is an element of
L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m})
.

Proof.

By Hölder’s inequality, we have
b\coloneqq Bu\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m})
, and therefore we can apply
Theorem

B.1
to obtain a unique solution
x\in W^{1,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n})
.
Applying then the generalized Hölder inequality (
Theorem

A.1
) to the output equation, we get
y=Cx+Du\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m})
.
∎

Next we study the regularity properties of the fundamental solution matrix associated to the homogeneous differential equation
\dot{x}=A(t)x
.
For that, we first need the following lemma.

Lemma B.3
.

Let
X:\mathbb{T}\to\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
and
X^{-1}:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{C}^{n,n},\ t\mapsto X(t)^{-1}
. Then the following statements hold:

1.

If
X\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
, then
X^{-1}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
.

2.

If
X\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
, then
X^{-1}\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
.

Proof.

Using that the inverse is
X^{-1}=\det(X)^{-1}\operatorname{adj}(X)
, where for all
t\in\mathbb{T}
,
\operatorname{adj}(X(t))\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
denotes the adjugate matrix of
X(t)
.

1.

Suppose that
X
is continuous. Then
\det(X)
and
\operatorname{adj}(X)
are also continuous. Since
\det(X(t))\neq 0
for all
t\in\mathbb{T}
, we have that also
\det(X(t))^{-1}
is continuous and thus,
X^{-1}=\det(X)^{-1}\operatorname{adj}(X)\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
.

2.

Due to
1
., we have that
X^{-1}\in\mathcal{C}\subseteq L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}\subseteq L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
.
From matrix differential calculus, we know that
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}(X^{-1})=X^{-1}\dot{X}X^{-1}
is the weak derivative of
X^{-1}
.
By the generalized Hölder inequality (
Theorem

A.1
) it follows that
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}(X^{-1})\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
, and therefore
X^{-1}\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
.
∎

We proceed by studying the properties of the fundamental solution matrix.

Theorem B.4
.

Let
A\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n,n})
. Then the following statements hold:

1.

For every
t_{0}\in\mathbb{T}
the homogeneous ordinary matrix differential equation
\dot{X}(t)=A(t)X(t)
has exactly one solution
X\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n,n})
such that
X(t_{0})=I_{n}
.

2.

For every
(t_{0},x_{0})\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}
the unique solution of the homogeneous differential equation
\dot{x}=A(t)x
that satisfies the initial condition
x(t_{0})=x_{0}
can be expressed as
x(t)=X(t)x_{0}
for all
t\in\mathbb{T}
.

3.

X(t)
is invertible for all
t\in\mathbb{T}
. In particular
X^{-1}\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n,n})
.

4.

For every
(t_{0},x_{0})\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}
the unique solution
x\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n})
of the inhomogeneous differential equation (
54
) that satisfies the initial condition
x(t_{0})=x_{0}
, can be expressed as

x(t)=X(t)\left(x_{0}+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}X^{-1}(s)b(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\right)

(55)

for all
t\in\mathbb{T}
.

Proof.

1.

The existence and uniqueness of the solution
X\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n,n})
follows immediately from
Theorem

B.1
, reinterpreting
\dot{X}=AX
as
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathrm{vec}(X)=(I_{n}\otimes A(t))\mathrm{vec}(X)
under vectorization, where
\otimes
represents the Kronecker product, and applying
Theorem

B.1
.

2.

Let
x(t)\coloneqq X(t)x_{0}
. Then
\dot{x}(t)=\dot{X}(t)x_{0}=A(t)X(t)x_{0}=A(t)x(t)
and
x(t_{0})=A(t_{0})x_{0}=x_{0}
.

3.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that there exists
t_{1}\in\mathbb{T}
for which
X(t_{1})
is singular, i.e. there is
x_{1}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
such that
X(t_{1})x_{1}=0
.
Because of
2
., both
x(t)\coloneqq X(t)x_{1}
and
\widetilde{x}(t)\equiv 0
are solutions of
\dot{x}(t)=A(t)x(t)
satisfying
x(t_{1})=\widetilde{x}(t_{1})=0
.
It follows that
x=\widetilde{x}
, and therefore
x_{1}=X(t_{0})x_{1}=x(t_{0})=\widetilde{x}(t_{0})=0
, in contradiction with
x_{1}\neq 0
.
Therefore,
X(t)
is invertible for all
t\in\mathbb{T}
, and by
Lemma

B.3
it follows that
X^{-1}\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
.

4.

Define

f:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{C}^{n},\qquad t\mapsto\int_{t_{0}}^{t}X^{-1}(s)b(s)\,\mathrm{d}s

and
\widetilde{x}\coloneqq x-Xf
.
By the generalized Hölder inequality (
Theorem

A.1
),
X^{-1}b\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},C^{n})
. Thus,
f\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n})
with
\dot{f}=X^{-1}b
, by
Lemma

A.2
.
Since
W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C})
is an algebra, we get from
X\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n})
that
\widetilde{x}=x-Xf\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n})
.
Furthermore, we obtain that

\dot{\widetilde{x}}=\dot{x}-\dot{X}f-X\dot{f}=Ax+b-AXf-XX^{-1}b=A(x-Xf)=A\widetilde{x}

and
\widetilde{x}(t_{0})=x_{0}
. Thus
\widetilde{x}(t)=X(t)x_{0}
for all
t\in\mathbb{T}
, because of (
2
).
We conclude that

x(t)=\widetilde{x}(t)+X(t)f(t)=X(t)x_{0}+X(t)\int_{t_{0}}^{t}X^{-1}(s)b(s)\,\mathrm{d}s=X(t)\left(x_{0}+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}X^{-1}(s)b(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\right)

holds for all
t\in\mathbb{T}
. ∎

Many of the properties stated in
Theorem

B.4
also apply to the state-transition matrix. We first need the following lemma.

Lemma B.5
.

Let
\mathbb{T}_{1},\mathbb{T}_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
be measurable sets and
u\in W^{1,1}(\mathbb{T}_{1}\times\mathbb{T}_{2},\mathbb{R})
(resp.
u\in W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,1}(\mathbb{T}_{1}\times\mathbb{T}_{2},\mathbb{R})
). Then
u_{s}\coloneqq u(\cdot,s)\in W^{1,1}(\mathbb{T}_{1},\mathbb{R})
(resp.
u_{s}\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T}_{1},\mathbb{R})
) with
\dot{u}_{s}=\frac{\partial u}{\partial t}(\cdot,s)
for a.e.
s\in\mathbb{T}_{2}
.

Proof.

Assume first that
u\in W^{1,1}(\mathbb{T}_{1}\times\mathbb{T}_{2},\mathbb{R})
.
By applying Fubini’s theorem
[
8
]
to
u\in L^{1}
and
\frac{\partial u}{\partial t}\in L^{1}
, we obtain that
u_{s}\in L^{1}
and
\dot{u}_{s}\coloneqq\frac{\partial u}{\partial t}(\cdot,s)\in L^{1}
for a.e.
s\in\mathbb{T}_{2}
.
For every
\varphi_{1}\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{T}_{1},\mathbb{R})
and
\varphi_{2}\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{T}_{2},\mathbb{R})
we have then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}_{2}}\varphi_{2}(s)\int_{\mathbb{T}_{1}}\frac{\partial u}{\partial t}(t,s)\varphi_{1}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}s=\iint_{\mathbb{T}_{1}\times\mathbb{T}_{2}}\frac{\partial u}{\partial t}(t,s)\varphi_{1}(t)\varphi_{2}(s)\,\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad=-\iint_{\mathbb{T}_{1}\times\mathbb{T}_{2}}u(t,s)\dot{\varphi}_{1}(t)\varphi_{2}(s)\,\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}s=-\int_{\mathbb{T}_{2}}\varphi_{2}(s)\int_{\mathbb{T}_{1}}u_{s}(t,s)\dot{\varphi}_{1}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}s,

thus

\int_{\mathbb{T}_{1}}\frac{\partial u}{\partial t}(t,s)\varphi_{1}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=-\int_{\mathbb{T}_{1}}u_{s}(t,s)\dot{\varphi}_{1}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t,

for a.e.
s\in\mathbb{T}_{2}
, and therefore
u_{s}\in W^{1,1}(\mathbb{T}_{1},\mathbb{R})
with
\dot{u}_{s}=\frac{\partial u}{\partial t}(\cdot,s)
.

Suppose now that
u\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T}_{1},\mathbb{T}_{2})
and let
K_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{T}_{1}
be a compact set.
Since
u_{s}|_{K_{1}}=u|_{K_{1}\times\{s\}}(\cdot,s)
for all
s\in\mathbb{T}_{2}
, and
u_{s}\in W^{1,1}(K_{1}\times\{s\},\mathbb{R})
, we conclude from the first part of the proof that
u_{s}|_{K_{1}}\in W^{1,1}(K_{1},\mathbb{R})
with
\dot{u}_{s}|_{K_{1}}=\frac{\partial u}{\partial t}(\cdot,s)|_{K_{1}}
for a.e.
s\in\mathbb{T}_{2}
.
∎

Theorem B.6
.

Let
A\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n,n})
. Then the partial differential equation

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Phi(t,s)=A(t)\Phi(t,s)\qquad\text{for }t,s\in\mathbb{T}

(56)

has exactly one solution
\Phi\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n,n})
such that
\Phi(s,s)=I_{n}
for all
s\in\mathbb{T}
.
Furthermore,
\Phi
satisfies the following properties:

1.

It holds that
\Phi(t,s)=X(t)X(s)^{-1}
for a.e.
t,s\in\mathbb{T}
, where
X\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
is any fundamental solution matrix of
\dot{X}(t)=A(t)X(t)
. In particular,
\Phi\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T},\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
.

2.

For every
(t_{0},x_{0})\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}
, the unique solution of the homogeneous differential equation
\dot{x}=A(t)x(t)
satisfying the initial condition
x(t_{0})=x_{0}
, can be expressed as
x(t)=\Phi(t,t_{0})x_{0}
for all
t\in\mathbb{T}
.

3.

For every
(t_{0},x_{0})\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}
the unique solution of the inhomogeneous differential equation (
54
) satisfying the initial condition
x(t_{0})=x_{0}
can be expressed as

x(t)=\Phi(t,t_{0})x_{0}+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\Phi(t,s)b(s)\,\mathrm{d}s

for all
t\in\mathbb{T}
.

4.

For every
t,s\in\mathbb{T}
the matrix
\Phi(t,s)\in\mathbb{C}^{n,n}
is invertible and
\Phi(t,s)^{-1}=\Phi(s,t)
. In particular,
\Phi^{-1}:\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{C}^{n,n}
is also a continuous
W^{1,1}
matrix function.

5.

\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\Phi(t,s)=\Phi(t,s)A(s)
holds for all
t,s\in\mathbb{T}
.

Proof.

We start by showing that, for every fundamental solution matrix
X\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
of
\dot{X}(t)=A(t)X(t)
,
\Phi(t,s)\coloneqq X(t)X(s)^{-1}
defines a solution of (
56
).
In fact, it holds that

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\big(X(t)X(s)^{-1}\big)=\dot{X}(t)X(s)^{-1}=A(t)X(t)X(s)^{-1}=A(t)\Phi(t,s)

and
\Phi(s,s)=X(s)X(s)^{-1}=I_{n}
for all
t,s\in\mathbb{T}
. Note that
\Phi\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T},\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
, since
X\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
.
To show that
\Phi\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T},\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
, we proceed as follows.
Let
K\subseteq\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}
be a compact set. Since the projections
\pi_{1}:\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{T},\ (t,s)\mapsto t
and
\pi_{2}:\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{T},\ (t,s)\mapsto s
are continuous maps,
K_{1}\coloneqq\pi_{1}(K)
and
K_{2}\coloneqq\pi_{2}(K)
are compact subsets of
\mathbb{T}
such that
K\subseteq K_{1}\times K_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}
. Let us set
Y\coloneqq\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}(X^{-1})\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
. Then,

\displaystyle\lVert\Phi\rVert_{W^{1,1}(K)}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\Phi\rVert_{W^{1,1}(K_{1}\times K_{2})}=\iint_{K_{1}\times K_{2}}\left(\lVert\Phi(t,s)\rVert+\left\lVert\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t}(t,s)\right\rVert+\left\lVert\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial s}(t,s)\right\rVert\right)\,\mathrm{d}(t\times s)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{K_{1}\times K_{2}}\left(\lVert X(t)\rVert\lVert Y(s)\rVert+\lVert\dot{X}(t)\rVert\lVert Y(s)\rVert+\lVert X(t)\rVert\left\lVert Y(s)\right\rVert\right)\,\mathrm{d}(t\times s)

\displaystyle=\int_{K_{1}}\lVert X(t)\rVert\,\mathrm{d}t\int_{K_{2}}\lVert Y(s)\rVert\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{K_{1}}\lVert\dot{X}(t)\rVert\,\mathrm{d}t\int_{K_{2}}\lVert Y(s)\rVert\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{K_{1}}\lVert X(t)\rVert\,\mathrm{d}t\int_{K_{2}}\lVert\dot{Y}(s)\rVert\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\lVert X\rVert_{L^{1}(K_{1})}\lVert Y\rVert_{L^{1}(K_{2})}+\lVert\dot{X}\rVert_{L^{1}(K_{1})}\lVert Y\rVert_{L^{1}(K_{2})}+\lVert X\rVert_{L^{1}(K_{1})}\lVert\dot{Y}\rVert_{L^{1}(K_{2})}<\infty.

We proceed to prove the other statements:

1.

It remains to show that the solution
\Phi
of (
56
) is unique.
Given any solution
\Phi
, define
X_{s}:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{C}^{n,n},\ t\mapsto\Phi(t,s)
for all fixed
s\in\mathbb{T}
. Because of
Lemma

B.5
,
X_{s}\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
is a solution of

\dot{X}_{s}(t)=A(t)X_{s}(t),\ X_{s}(s)=\Phi(s,s)=I_{n}

for a.e.
s\in\mathbb{T}
, i.e., it is the fundamental solution matrix of
\dot{X}(t)=A(t)X(t)
with
X(s)=I_{n}
.
This determines
\Phi
uniquely.

2.

Let
X\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
be the fundamental solution matrix of
\dot{X}(t)=A(t)X(t)
with
X(t_{0})=I_{n}
.
Due to
Theorem

B.4
, it holds that
\Phi(t,t_{0})x_{0}=X(t)X(t_{0})^{-1}x_{0}=X(t)x_{0}=x(t)
for all
t\in\mathbb{T}
.

3.

From (
55
) and
X(t_{0})=I_{n}
we deduce that

x(t)=X(t)X^{-1}(t_{0})x_{0}+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}X(t)X^{-1}(s)b(s)\,\mathrm{d}s=\Phi(t,t_{0})x_{0}+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\Phi(t,s)b(s)\,\mathrm{d}s

holds for all
t\in\mathbb{T}
.

4.

For every
t,s\in\mathbb{T}
, we have
\Phi(t,s)^{-1}=(X(t)X(s)^{-1})^{-1}=X(s)X(t)^{-1}=\Phi(s,t)
.
Since
\Phi^{-1}
is the composition of
\Phi
with the transposition of its two arguments, it is clear that it preserves continuity, differentiability, and integrability properties.

5.

We have

\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\Phi(t,s)=\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\big(X(t)X(s)^{-1}\big)=X(t)X(s)^{-1}\dot{X}(s)X(s)^{-1}=\Phi(t,s)A(s)

for every
t,s\in\mathbb{T}
.
∎

We now switch our focus to the existence of solutions of Riccati differential equations, see
[
1
,
47
]
for an extensive analysis.

Theorem B.7
.

Let
A\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n,n})
,
F,G\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}(\mathbb{C}))
,
t_{0}\in\mathbb{T}
, and
Q_{0}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}(\mathbb{C})
.
Then there exists
\delta>0
such that the Riccati differential equation

\dot{Q}+{A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q+QA+QFQ+G=0

(57)

has a unique local solution
Q\in W^{1,1}((t_{0}-\delta,t_{0}+\delta),\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}(\mathbb{C}))
such that
Q(t_{0})=Q_{0}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathbb{T}
is open, the inclusion
\mathbb{T}_{0}\coloneqq(t_{0}-\delta,t_{0}+\delta)\subseteq\mathbb{T}
holds for arbitrary small
\delta>0
.
Due to
Lemma

A.2
, up to choosing
\delta>0
sufficiently small, we can ensure that
\lVert A\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}_{0})}
,
\lVert F\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}_{0})}
, and
\lVert G\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}_{0})}
are arbitrarily small. In particular, let us impose

\lVert A\rVert_{L^{1}}\leq\max\left(\frac{1}{6(\lVert Q_{0}\rVert+1)},\ \frac{1}{8}\right),\quad\lVert F\rVert_{L^{1}}\leq\max\left(\frac{1}{3(\lVert Q_{0}\rVert+1)^{2}},\ \frac{1}{8(\lVert Q_{0}\rVert+1)}\right),\quad\lVert G\rVert_{L^{1}}\leq\frac{1}{3}.

Let us restrict our search to functions
Q\in W^{1,1}(\mathbb{T}_{0},\mathcal{B})
, where

\mathcal{B}\coloneqq\left\{S\in\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}(\mathbb{C})\mid\lVert S-Q_{0}\rVert_{2}\leq 1\right\}.

Every solution
Q
of (
57
) has to satisfy the integral equation

Q(t)=Q_{0}-\int_{t_{0}}^{t}({A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q+QA+QFQ+G)\,\mathrm{d}s

(58)

for all
t\in\mathbb{T}_{0}
, or equivalently
Q=T(Q)
, where

T:L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}_{0},\mathcal{B})\to L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}_{0},\mathcal{B}),\quad T(Q)(t)\coloneqq Q_{0}-\int_{t_{0}}^{t}({A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q+QA+QFQ+G)\,\mathrm{d}s.

Note that
T
is well-defined, since

\displaystyle\lVert T(Q)(t)-Q_{0}\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\big(2\lVert A(s)\rVert\lVert Q(s)\rVert+\lVert F(s)\rVert\lVert Q(s)\rVert^{2}+\lVert G\rVert\big)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq 2\lVert A\rVert_{L^{1}}(\lVert Q_{0}\rVert+1)+\lVert F\rVert_{L^{1}}(\lVert Q_{0}\rVert+1)^{2}+\lVert G\rVert_{L^{1}}<\frac{1}{3}+\frac{1}{3}+\frac{1}{3}=1

for all
t\in\mathbb{T}_{0}
.
Furthermore, note that
W^{1,1}(\mathbb{T}_{0},\mathcal{B})\subseteq L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}_{0},\mathcal{B})
, and that
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}_{0},\mathcal{B})
is a Banach space, since it is a closed subspace of
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}_{0},\mathbb{C}^{n,n})
.
For every
Q,\widetilde{Q}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}_{0},\mathcal{B})
and
t\in\mathbb{T}_{0}
it then holds that

\displaystyle\lVert T(Q)(t)-T(\widetilde{Q})(t)\rVert=\left\lVert\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\big({A}^{\mathrm{H}}(\widetilde{Q}-Q)+(\widetilde{Q}-Q)A+(\widetilde{Q}-Q)F\widetilde{Q}+QF(\widetilde{Q}-Q)\big)\,\mathrm{d}s\right\rVert

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\big(2\lVert A(s)\rVert\lVert\widetilde{Q}(s)-Q(s)\rVert+\lVert F(s)\rVert\lVert\widetilde{Q}(s)-Q(s)\rVert(\lVert Q(s)\rVert+\lVert\widetilde{Q}(s)\rVert)\big)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\lVert\widetilde{Q}-Q\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\big(2\lVert A\rVert_{L^{1}}+2\lVert F\rVert_{L^{1}}(\lVert Q_{0}\rVert+1)\big)\leq\lVert\widetilde{Q}-Q\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\left(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{4}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\lVert\widetilde{Q}-Q\rVert_{L^{\infty}},

and therefore
T
is a contraction. We can then apply the Banach fixed point theorem, see e.g.
[
8
, Theorem 5.7]
to show that
T
has a unique fixed point
Q\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}_{0},\mathcal{B})
, which is the limit of the sequence
Q^{(k+1)}=T(Q^{(k)}),\ Q^{(0)}=Q_{0}
, for
k\to\infty
.
In particular, the integral equation (
58
) has
Q\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}_{0},\mathcal{B})
as its unique local solution.

To prove that
Q\in W^{1,1}(\mathbb{T}_{0},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}(\mathbb{C}))
, we observe that
K\coloneqq{A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q+QA+QFQ+G\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T}_{0},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}(\mathbb{C}))
because of the generalized Hölder inequality (
Theorem

A.1
), thus
L:t\mapsto\int_{t_{0}}^{t}K(s)\,\mathrm{d}s
satisfies
L\in W^{1,1}(\mathbb{T}_{0},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}(\mathbb{C}))
due to
Lemma

A.2
, and therefore
Q=Q_{0}+L\in W^{1,1}(\mathbb{T}_{0},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}(\mathbb{C}))
.
Due to
Lemma

A.2
we further obtain that

\dot{Q}=\dot{L}={A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q+QA+QFQ+G,

thus
Q
is indeed a local solution of (
57
), and uniqueness follows from the one of the solutions of (
58
).
∎

If the quadratic term in (
57
) is zero, then we have a Lyapunov differential equation. In that case, we are able to prove that its solution is global, and it can be expressed explicitly in terms of the state-transition matrix associated to some LTV system.

Theorem B.8
.

Let
A\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n,n})
,
C\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}(\mathbb{C}))
,
t_{0}\in\mathbb{T}
, and
Q_{0}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}(\mathbb{C})
.
Then the initial value problem

\dot{Q}(t)={A}^{\mathrm{H}}(t)Q(t)+Q(t)A(t)+G(t),\qquad Q(t_{0})=Q_{0}

(59)

has the unique global solution

Q(t)=\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,t_{0})Q_{0}{\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,t_{0})}^{\mathrm{H}}+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,s)G(s){\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,s)}^{\mathrm{H}}\,\mathrm{d}s,

(60)

where
\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
denotes the state-transition matrix associated to
\dot{x}={A}^{\mathrm{H}}x
.

Proof.

The existence of a unique local solution follows immediately from
Theorem

B.7
with
F=0
.
Note that
Q
defined as in (
60
) satisfies
Q(t_{0})=Q_{0}
and is in
W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n,n})
, by construction.
By the properties of differentiation, we obtain then

\displaystyle\dot{Q}(t)

\displaystyle=\dot{\Phi}_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,t_{0})Q_{0}{\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,t_{0})}^{\mathrm{H}}+\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,t_{0})Q_{0}{\dot{\Phi}_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,t_{0})}^{\mathrm{H}}+\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,t)C(t)\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,t)\penalty\ +

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\big(\dot{\Phi}_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,s)G(s){\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,s)}^{\mathrm{H}}+\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,s)G(s){\dot{\Phi}_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,s)}^{\mathrm{H}}\big)\,\mathrm{d}s=

\displaystyle={A(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,t_{0})Q_{0}{\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,t_{0})}^{\mathrm{H}}+\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,t_{0})Q_{0}\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,t_{0})A(t)+G(t)\penalty\ +

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\big({A(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,s)G(s){\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,s)}^{\mathrm{H}}+\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,s)G(s){\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,s)}^{\mathrm{H}}A(t)\big)\,\mathrm{d}s=

\displaystyle={A(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}\left(\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,t_{0})Q_{0}{\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,t_{0})}^{\mathrm{H}}+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,s)G(s){\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,s)}^{\mathrm{H}}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)\penalty\ +

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,t_{0})Q_{0}{\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,t_{0})}^{\mathrm{H}}+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,s)G(s){\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,s)}^{\mathrm{H}}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)A(t)+G(t)=

\displaystyle={A(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}Q(t)+Q(t)A(t)+G(t),

thus
Q
is in fact the unique solution of (
59
).
∎

Finally, we study the solutions of a matrix integral equation, that resembles a Lyapunov equation but with an additional term.

Corollary B.9
.

Let
A\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n,n})
,
L\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}(\mathbb{C}))
,
M\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}(\mathbb{C}))
,
t_{0}\in\mathbb{T}
, and
Q_{0}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}(\mathbb{C})
. Then the integral equation

Q(t)=Q(t_{0})+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\big({A(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}Q(s)+Q(s)A(s)+L(s)\big)\,\mathrm{d}s+M(t)-M(t_{0}),\qquad Q(t_{0})=t_{0}

(61)

has the unique global solution
Q\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}(\mathbb{C}))
of the form

\begin{split}Q(t)&=M(t)+\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,t_{0})\big(Q_{0}-M(t_{0})\big){\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,t_{0})}^{\mathrm{H}}\penalty\ +\\
&\qquad+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,s)\big(L(s)+{A}^{\mathrm{H}}(s)M(s)+M(s)A(s)\big){\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,s)}^{\mathrm{H}}\,\mathrm{d}s,\end{split}

(62)

where
\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
denotes the state-transition matrix associated to
\dot{x}={A}^{\mathrm{H}}x
.

Proof.

Defining
\widetilde{Q}\coloneqq Q-M
, there is an obvious bijection between the solutions of (
61
) and the ones of

\widetilde{Q}(t)=\widetilde{Q}(t_{0})+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\left({A(s)}^{\mathrm{H}}\widetilde{Q}(s)+\widetilde{Q}(s)A(s)+L(s)+{A(s)}^{\mathrm{H}}M(s)+M(s)A(s)\right)\,\mathrm{d}s,

with
\widetilde{Q}(t_{0})=Q(t_{0})-M(t_{0})
, which are exactly the solutions of the ordinary differential matrix equation

\dot{\widetilde{Q}}(t)={A(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}\widetilde{Q}(t)+\widetilde{Q}(t)A(t)+\widetilde{L}(t),\qquad\widetilde{Q}(t_{0})=Q_{0}-M(t_{0}),

(63)

with
\widetilde{L}\coloneqq L+{A}^{\mathrm{H}}M+MA\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}(\mathbb{C}))
.
It then immediately follows from
Theorem

B.8
that (
63
) has a unique solution of the form

\widetilde{Q}(t)=\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,t_{0})\big(Q_{0}-M(t_{0})\big){\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,t_{0})}^{\mathrm{H}}+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,s)\widetilde{L}(s){\Phi_{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}(t,s)}^{\mathrm{H}}\,\mathrm{d}s,

and by setting
Q(t)=\widetilde{Q}(t)+M(t)
and
\widetilde{L}(s)=L(s)+{A(s)}^{\mathrm{H}}M(s)+M(s)A(s)
we obtain (
62
).
∎

Appendix C
System transformations

In this section, we prove the invariance of the disspativity properties under transformations presented in
Theorem

2.9
.

C.1
State transformations

Since the input and output variables are unchanged under state space transformations, it is clear that the property of a system to have nonnegative supply
(NN)
is invariant.
It is also clear that the passive property
(Pa)
is invariant under state space transformations, up to the replacement of a storage function
V:\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
with

\widetilde{V}:\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\mathbb{R},\ (t,\widetilde{x})\mapsto V(t,Z(t)\widetilde{x}).

It is somewhat more involved to show that the properties of admitting a
(KYP)
solution and a
(pH)
representation are also invariant under state-space transformations.

Lemma C.1
.

Let
Z\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
be a state transformation applied to a LTV system of the form (
1
). A matrix function
Q\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
is a solution of the
(KYP)
inequality (
4
) if and only if
\widetilde{Q}\coloneqq{Z}^{\mathrm{H}}QZ\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
is a solution of the KYP inequality

\begin{bmatrix}-{\widetilde{A}}^{\mathrm{H}}\widetilde{Q}-\widetilde{Q}\widetilde{A}-\dot{\widetilde{Q}}&{\widetilde{C}}^{\mathrm{H}}-\widetilde{Q}\widetilde{B}\\
\widetilde{C}-{\widetilde{B}}^{\mathrm{H}}\widetilde{Q}&D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}}\end{bmatrix}\geq 0

of (
9
).

Proof.

Suppose that
Q
is a solution of the KYP inequality (
4
). By construction,
\widetilde{Q}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C})
and

\begin{bmatrix}-\widetilde{{A}^{\mathrm{H}}}\widetilde{Q}-\widetilde{Q}{\widetilde{A}}^{\mathrm{H}}-\dot{\widetilde{Q}}&{\widetilde{C}}^{\mathrm{H}}-\widetilde{Q}\widetilde{B}\\
\widetilde{C}-{\widetilde{B}}^{\mathrm{H}}\widetilde{Q}&D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}}\end{bmatrix}={\begin{bmatrix}Z&0\\
0&I_{m}\end{bmatrix}}^{\mathrm{H}}\begin{bmatrix}-{A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q-QA-\dot{Q}&{C}^{\mathrm{H}}-QB\\
C-{B}^{\mathrm{H}}Q&D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}Z&0\\
0&I_{m}\end{bmatrix}\geq 0.

Since
Z
is pointwise invertible, the converse statement follows analogously.
∎

Lemma C.2
.

Let
Z\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
be a state transformation applied to a LTV system of the form (
1
). The system (
1
) admits a pH representation of the form (
2
) if and only if the transformed system (
9
) admits a pH representation of the form

\begin{bmatrix}\widetilde{A}&\widetilde{B}\\
\widetilde{C}&D\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\big(\widetilde{J}-\widetilde{R}\big)\widetilde{Q}-\widetilde{K}&\widetilde{G}-\widetilde{P}\\
\big({\widetilde{G}}^{\mathrm{H}}+{\widetilde{P}}^{\mathrm{H}}\big)\widetilde{Q}&S-N\end{bmatrix},

(64)

where
\widetilde{K}=Z^{-1}(\dot{Z}+KZ)
,
\widetilde{J}=Z^{-1}J{Z}^{-\mathrm{H}}
,
\widetilde{R}=Z^{-1}R{Z}^{-\mathrm{H}}
,
\widetilde{Q}={Z}^{\mathrm{H}}QZ
,
\widetilde{G}=Z^{-1}G
, and
\widetilde{P}=Z^{-1}P
.

Proof.

Suppose that (
1
) admits a pH representation of the form (
2
).
Note first that

\displaystyle(\widetilde{J}-\widetilde{R})\widetilde{Q}-\widetilde{K}=Z^{-1}(J-R)QZ-Z^{-1}(\dot{Z}+KZ)=Z^{-1}(AZ-\dot{Z})=\widetilde{A},

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}-\widetilde{P}=Z^{-1}(G-P)=Z^{-1}B=\widetilde{B},

\displaystyle{(\widetilde{G}+\widetilde{P})}^{\mathrm{H}}\widetilde{Q}={(G+P)}^{\mathrm{H}}QZ=CZ=\widetilde{C}

holds, i.e., the coefficient representation (
64
) is well-defined.
It is also clear that all matrix functions retain their regularity, that
\widetilde{Q}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C})
, and that

\displaystyle\widetilde{Q}\widetilde{K}+{\widetilde{K}}^{\mathrm{H}}\widetilde{Q}

\displaystyle={Z}^{\mathrm{H}}Q(\dot{Z}+KZ)+{(\dot{Z}+KZ)}^{\mathrm{H}}QZ=

\displaystyle={Z}^{\mathrm{H}}Q\dot{Z}+{Z}^{\mathrm{H}}(QK+{K}^{\mathrm{H}}Q)Z+{\dot{Z}}^{\mathrm{H}}Q\dot{Z}=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}({Z}^{\mathrm{H}}QZ)=\dot{\widetilde{Q}}.

Finally, it holds that

\widetilde{W}\coloneqq\begin{bmatrix}\widetilde{R}&\widetilde{P}\\
{\widetilde{P}}^{\mathrm{H}}&S\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}Z^{-1}&0\\
0&I_{m}\end{bmatrix}W{\begin{bmatrix}Z^{-1}&0\\
0&I_{m}\end{bmatrix}}^{\mathrm{H}},

in particular
\widetilde{W}(t)\in\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n+m}_{+}(\mathbb{C})
for a.e.
t\in\mathbb{T}
.
Therefore, (
64
) is a pH system with the given representation.
Since
Z
is pointwise invertible, the converse statement follows analogously.
∎

C.2
Input-output transformations

If we apply a change of input-output variables of the form
\check{u}=V^{-1}u
,
\check{y}={V}^{\mathrm{H}}y
with
V,V^{-1}\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{GL}_{m}(\mathbb{C}))
, it is clear that
{\check{y}}^{\mathrm{H}}\check{u}={y}^{\mathrm{H}}u
. It immediately follows that the properties of having a nonnegative supply
(NN)
and of being passive
(Pa)
are invariant under this transformation, with the same storage functions.
We explicitly prove the invariance of
(pH)
and
(KYP)
:

Lemma C.3
.

Consider an LTV system of the form (
1
) and a change of input-output variables
V,V^{-1}\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{GL}_{m}(\mathbb{C}))
.
Then
Q\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
is a solution of the
(KYP)
inequality (
4
) if and only if it is also a solution of the KYP inequality

\begin{bmatrix}-{A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q-QA-\dot{Q}&{\check{C}}^{\mathrm{H}}-Q\check{B}\\
\check{C}-{\check{B}}^{\mathrm{H}}Q&\check{D}+{\check{D}}^{\mathrm{H}}\end{bmatrix}\geq 0

associated to the transformed system (
10
).

Proof.

Suppose that
Q\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n}_{+}(\mathbb{C}))
is a solution of the KYP inequality (
4
). Then

\begin{bmatrix}-{A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q-QA-\dot{Q}&{\check{C}}^{\mathrm{H}}-Q\check{B}\\
\check{C}-{\check{B}}^{\mathrm{H}}Q&\check{D}+{\check{D}}^{\mathrm{H}}\end{bmatrix}={\begin{bmatrix}I_{n}&0\\
0&V\end{bmatrix}}^{\mathrm{H}}\begin{bmatrix}-{A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q-QA-\dot{Q}&{C}^{\mathrm{H}}-QB\\
C-{B}^{\mathrm{H}}Q&D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}I_{n}&0\\
0&V\end{bmatrix}\geq 0

holds, by construction. Since
V
is a.e. invertible, the converse statement is proven analogously.
∎

Lemma C.4
.

Consider an LTV system of the form (
1
) and a change of input-output variables
V,V^{-1}\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{GL}_{m}(\mathbb{C}))
.
Then the LTV system (
1
) has a
(pH)
formulation of the form (
18
) if and only if

\begin{bmatrix}A&\check{B}\\
\check{C}&\check{D}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}(J-R)Q-K&\check{G}-\check{P}\\
{(\check{G}+\check{P})}^{\mathrm{H}}Q&\check{S}-\check{N}\end{bmatrix}

(65)

with
\check{G}=GV
,
\check{P}=PV
,
\check{S}={V}^{\mathrm{H}}SV
, and
\check{N}={V}^{\mathrm{H}}NV
is a
(pH)
formulation for the transformed system (
10
).

Proof.

Suppose that (
1
) admits a pH representation of the form (
18
). By construction, we have that

\begin{bmatrix}(J-R)Q-K&\check{G}-\check{P}\\
{(\check{G}+\check{P})}^{\mathrm{H}}Q&\check{S}-\check{N}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}A&(G-P)V\\
{V}^{\mathrm{H}}{(G+P)}^{\mathrm{H}}Q&{V}^{\mathrm{H}}(S-N)V\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}A&BV\\
{V}^{\mathrm{H}}C&{V}^{\mathrm{H}}DV\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}A&\check{B}\\
\check{C}&\check{D}\end{bmatrix},

i.e., (
65
) holds.
Furthermore, it is clear that the properties
J(t)=-{J(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}
,
\check{N}(t)=-{N(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}
,
Q(t)K(t)+{K(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}Q(t)=\dot{Q}(t)
and

\check{W}(t)\coloneqq\begin{bmatrix}R(t)&\check{P}(t)\\
{\check{P}(t)}^{\mathrm{H}}&\check{S}(t)\end{bmatrix}={\begin{bmatrix}I_{n}&0\\
0&V(t)\end{bmatrix}}^{\mathrm{H}}W(t)\begin{bmatrix}I_{n}&0\\
0&V(t)\end{bmatrix}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{S}}^{n+m}_{+}(\mathbb{C})

hold for a.e.
t\in\mathbb{T}
, thus
Q,K,J,R,\check{G},\check{P},\check{S},\check{N}
define a pH formulation for (
10
).
Since
V
is a.e. invertible, the converse statement is proven analogously.
∎

C.3
Time transformations

An immediate consequence of
Lemma

2.8
is that the property of having a nonnegative supply
(NN)
is invariant under time transformations.
Similarly, passivity
(Pa)
is also invariant under time transformations, up to replacing a storage function
V:\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\widehat{V}:\widehat{\mathbb{T}}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\mathbb{R},\ (\widehat{t},\widehat{x})\mapsto V(\theta(\widehat{t}),\widehat{x})
. It is again more involved to show that the properties of admitting a
(KYP)
solution and a
(pH)
representation are invariant under time transformations.

Lemma C.5
.

Given a system of the form (
1
) and a diffeomorphism
\theta\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{T}},\mathbb{T})
with
\dot{\theta}>0
pointwise, a matrix function
Q\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n,n})
satisfying
Q={Q}^{\mathrm{H}}\geq 0
pointwise is a solution of the KYP inequality (
4
) of (
1
) if and only if
\widehat{Q}\coloneqq Q\circ\theta\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\widehat{\mathbb{T}},\mathbb{C}^{n,n})
is a solution of the KYP inequality

\begin{bmatrix}(-{\widehat{A}}^{\mathrm{H}}\widehat{Q}-\widehat{Q}\widehat{A}-\dot{\widehat{Q}})(\widehat{t})&({\widehat{C}}^{\mathrm{H}}-\widehat{Q}\widehat{B})(\widehat{t})\\
(\widehat{C}-{\widehat{B}}^{\mathrm{H}}\widehat{Q})(\widehat{t})&(\widehat{D}+{\widehat{D}}^{\mathrm{H}})(\widehat{t})\end{bmatrix}\geq 0

(66)

of (
9
), for a.e.
\widehat{t}\in\widehat{\mathbb{T}}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
Q
is a solution of the KYP inequality of (
1
).
Note first that trivially
\widehat{Q}={\widehat{Q}}^{\mathrm{H}}\geq 0
pointwise.
Since
\dot{\widehat{Q}}=\dot{\theta}(Q\circ\theta)
and
\dot{\theta}>0
pointwise, we immediately deduce that

\begin{bmatrix}-{\widehat{A}}^{\mathrm{H}}\widehat{Q}-\widehat{Q}\widehat{A}-\dot{\widehat{Q}}&{\widehat{C}}^{\mathrm{H}}-\widehat{Q}\widehat{B}\\
\widehat{C}-{\widehat{B}}^{\mathrm{H}}\widehat{Q}&\widehat{D}+{\widehat{D}}^{\mathrm{H}}\end{bmatrix}=\dot{\theta}\left(\begin{bmatrix}-{A}^{\mathrm{H}}Q-QA-\dot{Q}&{C}^{\mathrm{H}}-QB\\
C-{B}^{\mathrm{H}}Q&D+{D}^{\mathrm{H}}\end{bmatrix}\circ\theta\right)\geq 0,

i.e.,
\widehat{Q}
is a solution of (
66
).
Since
\theta
is a diffeomorphism and
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}(\theta^{-1})=1/(\dot{\theta}\circ\theta^{-1})>0
on
\mathbb{T}
, the converse statement can be proven analogously.
∎

Lemma C.6
.

Consider a system of the form (
1
) and a diffeomorphism
\theta\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{T}},\mathbb{T})
with
\dot{\theta}>0
pointwise. Then the system (
1
) admits a pH representation of the form (
2
) if and only if the transformed system (
11
) admits a pH representation of the form

\begin{bmatrix}\widehat{A}&\widehat{B}\\
\widehat{C}&\widehat{D}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\big(\widehat{J}-\widehat{R}\big)\widehat{Q}-\widehat{K}&\widehat{G}-\widehat{P}\\
\big({\widehat{G}}^{\mathrm{H}}+{\widehat{P}}^{\mathrm{H}}\big)\widehat{Q}&\widehat{S}-\widehat{N}\end{bmatrix},

(67)

where
\widehat{Q}=Q\circ\theta
and
\widehat{\square}=\dot{\theta}(\square\circ\theta)
for
\square\in\{J,R,K,G,P,S,N\}
.

Proof.

Suppose that (
1
) admits a pH representation of the form (
2
).
Note first that

\displaystyle(\widehat{J}-\widehat{R})\widehat{Q}-\widehat{K}=\dot{\theta}\big((J\circ\theta)-(R\circ\theta)\big)(Q\circ\theta)-\dot{\theta}(K\circ\theta)=\dot{\theta}(A\circ\theta)=\widehat{A},

\displaystyle\widehat{G}-\widehat{P}=\dot{\theta}(G\circ\theta)-\dot{\theta}(P\circ\theta)=\dot{\theta}(B\circ\theta)=\widehat{B},

\displaystyle{(\widehat{G}+\widehat{P})}^{\mathrm{H}}\widehat{Q}=\dot{\theta}{(G\circ\theta)+(P\circ\theta)}^{\mathrm{H}}(Q\circ\theta)=\dot{\theta}(C\circ\theta)=\widehat{C},

\displaystyle\widehat{S}-\widehat{N}=\dot{\theta}(S\circ\theta)-\dot{\theta}(N\circ\theta)=\dot{\theta}(D\circ\theta)=\widehat{D}

holds, i.e., the coefficient representation (
67
) is well-defined.
It is also clear that all matrix functions retain their regularity, that
\widehat{Q}={\widehat{Q}}^{\mathrm{H}}\geq 0
by construction, and that

\widehat{Q}\widehat{K}+{\widehat{K}}^{\mathrm{H}}\widehat{Q}=\dot{\theta}\big((Q\circ\theta)(K\circ\theta)+(K\circ\theta)^{\top}(Q\circ\theta)\big)=\dot{\theta}(\dot{Q}\circ\theta)=\dot{\widehat{Q}}.

Finally, it holds that

\widehat{W}\coloneqq\begin{bmatrix}\widehat{R}&\widehat{P}\\
{\widehat{P}}^{\mathrm{H}}&\widehat{S}\end{bmatrix}=\dot{\theta}\begin{bmatrix}R&P\\
{P}^{\mathrm{H}}&S\end{bmatrix}\circ\theta,

in particular
\widehat{W}={\widehat{W}}^{\mathrm{H}}\geq 0
almost everywhere.
Therefore, (
11
) is a pH system with the given representation.
Since
\theta
is invertible, the converse statement can be proven analogously.
∎

Appendix D
The Riemann-Stieltjes integral

We recall the Riemann-Stieltjes integral and its main properties; see e.g.
[
11
]
for an overview in the real-valued case.
In this section,
[t_{0},t_{1}]
always denotes a fixed compact real interval, unless otherwise specified.

Definition D.1
.

Given two maps
f,g:[t_{0},t_{1}]\to\mathbb{C}
, the
Riemann-Stieltjes integral
of
f
with respect to
g
over
[t_{0},t_{1}]
is defined (when it exists) as

\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}f(t)\,\mathrm{d}g(t)\coloneqq\lim_{\lVert\mathfrak{p}\rVert\to 0}\sum_{k=1}^{K}f(\widetilde{s}_{k})\big(g(\widetilde{t}_{k})-g(\widetilde{t}_{k-1})\big),

(68)

where for every partition
\mathfrak{p}=\{\widetilde{t}_{0},\ldots,\widetilde{t}_{K}\}
of
[t_{0},t_{1}]
we take any
\widetilde{s}_{k}\in[\widetilde{t}_{k-1},\widetilde{t}_{k}]
, for
k=1,\ldots,K
.
If the Riemann-Stieltjes integral exists, we write
f\in\mathcal{R}_{g}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C})
, or in the short notation
f\in\mathcal{R}_{g}
, when
[t_{0},t_{1}]
and
\mathbb{C}
are clear from the context.

Note that, for the Riemann-Stieltjes integral to be well defined, the convergence of the limit should be independent of the choice of
\{\widetilde{t}_{k}\}
and
\{\widetilde{s}_{k}\}
.
We define implicitly

\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\,\mathrm{d}g(t)\coloneqq\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}1\,\,\mathrm{d}g(t)=g(t_{1})-g(t_{0}),

by setting
f:t\mapsto 1
, obtaining something similar to the fundamental theorem of calculus.

We recall now several fundamental properties of the Riemann-Stieltjes integral without providing their proof, see, e.g.
[
11
]
.

Theorem D.2
.

For every
g:[t_{0},t_{1}]\to\mathbb{C}
, the set
\mathcal{R}_{g}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C})
is a vector space and an algebra.

Theorem D.3
.

Let
g\in\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{C}
and
f\in\mathcal{R}_{g}
. Then
g\in\mathcal{R}_{f}
and the integration by parts formula

f(t_{1})g(t_{1})-f(t_{0})g(t_{0})=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}f(t)\,\mathrm{d}g(t)+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}g(t)\,\mathrm{d}f(t)

(69)

holds.

Theorem D.4
.

If
f\in\mathcal{C}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C})
and
g\in\BV([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C})
, then
f\in\mathcal{R}_{g}
,
g\in\mathcal{R}_{f}
, and

\left\lvert\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}f(t)\,\mathrm{d}g(t)\right\rvert\leq\lVert f\rVert_{L^{\infty}([t_{0},t_{1}])}{\operatorname{Var}}_{t_{0},t_{1}}(g).

Theorem D.5
.

If
f\in\mathcal{C}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C})
and
g\in W^{1,1}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C})
, then

\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}f(t)\,\mathrm{d}g(t)=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}f(t)\dot{g}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t.

Before focusing on the matrix-valued case, we show that under some additional regularity assumptions, (
69
) can be extended to the product of three functions.

Lemma D.6
.

Let
f,g,h\in\BV([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C})
and suppose that any two among
f,g,h
are continuous. Then
fg\in\mathcal{R}_{h}
,
fh\in\mathcal{R}_{g}
,
gh\in\mathcal{R}_{f}
and the formula

f(t_{1})g(t_{1})h(t_{1})-f(t_{0})g(t_{0})h(t_{0})=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}f(t)g(t)\,\mathrm{d}h(t)+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}f(t)h(t)\,\mathrm{d}g(t)+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}g(t)h(t)\,\mathrm{d}f(t)

(70)

holds.

Proof.

Due to the obvious symmetry of the statement, it is sufficient to prove it for
f,g\in\mathcal{C}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C})
.
Since
fg\in\mathcal{C}
and
h\in\BV
,
g\in\mathcal{C}
and
fh\in\BV
, and
f\in\mathcal{C}
and
gh\in\BV
, we deduce from
Theorem

D.4
that
fg\in\mathcal{R}_{h}
,
fh\in\mathcal{R}_{g}
and
gh\in\mathcal{R}_{f}
.

For
\varepsilon>0
arbitrarily small, let
\delta>0
be such that, for all partitions
\mathfrak{p}=\{\widetilde{t}_{0},\ldots,\widetilde{t}_{K}\}\in\mathfrak{P}_{[t_{0},t_{1}]}
with
\lVert\mathfrak{p}\rVert<\delta
and every choice of
\widetilde{s}_{k}\in[\widetilde{t}_{k-1},\widetilde{t}_{k}]
for
k=1,\ldots,K
, it holds that
\lvert D_{1}\rvert,\lvert D_{2}\rvert,\lvert D_{3}\rvert<\varepsilon
, where

\displaystyle D_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}f(t)g(t)\,\mathrm{d}h(t)-\sum_{k=1}^{K}f(\widetilde{s}_{k})g(\widetilde{s}_{k})\big((h(\widetilde{t}_{k})-h(\widetilde{t}_{k-1})\big),

\displaystyle D_{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}f(t)h(t)\,\mathrm{d}g(t)-\sum_{k=1}^{K}f(\widetilde{s}_{k})h(\widetilde{s}_{k})\big((g(\widetilde{t}_{k})-g(\widetilde{t}_{k-1})\big),

\displaystyle D_{3}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}g(t)h(t)\,\mathrm{d}f(t)-\sum_{k=1}^{K}g(\widetilde{s}_{k})h(\widetilde{s}_{k})\big((f(\widetilde{t}_{k})-f(\widetilde{t}_{k-1})\big).

Up to selecting a smaller
\delta
, we can also suppose that
\lvert f(t)-f(s)\rvert<\varepsilon
for every
s,t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
with
\lvert t-s\rvert<\delta
, since
f
is uniformly continuous.
Let us now denote
f_{k}\coloneqq f(\widetilde{t}_{k})
,
g_{k}\coloneqq g(\widetilde{t}_{k})
and
h_{k}\coloneqq h(\widetilde{t}_{k})
for
k=0,\ldots,K
. Then we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}f_{k}g_{k}(h_{k}-h_{k-1})+\sum_{k=1}^{K}f_{k-1}h_{k-1}(g_{k}-g_{k-1})+\sum_{k=1}^{K}g_{k-1}h_{k-1}(f_{k}-f_{k-1})

\displaystyle=f_{K}g_{K}h_{K}+\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}f_{k}g_{k}h_{k}-\sum_{k=1}^{K}f_{k}g_{k}h_{k-1}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}f_{k-1}g_{k}h_{k-1}

\displaystyle\qquad-f_{0}g_{0}h_{0}-\sum_{k=2}^{K}f_{k-1}g_{k-1}h_{k-1}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}f_{k}g_{k-1}h_{k-1}-\sum_{k=1}^{K}f_{k-1}g_{k-1}h_{k-1}

\displaystyle=f_{K}g_{K}h_{K}-f_{0}g_{0}h_{0}-\sum_{k=1}^{K}(f_{k}g_{k}-f_{k-1}g_{k}-f_{k}g_{k-1}+f_{k-1}g_{k-1})h_{k-1}

\displaystyle=f(t_{1})g(t_{1})h(t_{1})-f(t_{0})g(t_{0})h(t_{0})-\sum_{k=1}^{K}(f_{k}-f_{k-1})(g_{k}-g_{k-1})h_{k-1},

and therefore

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}f(t)g(t)\,\mathrm{d}h(t)+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}f(t)h(t)\,\mathrm{d}g(t)+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}g(t)h(t)\,\mathrm{d}f(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}f_{k}g_{k}(h_{k}-h_{k-1})+\sum_{k=1}^{K}f_{k-1}h_{k-1}(g_{k}-g_{k-1})+\sum_{k=1}^{K}g_{k-1}h_{k-1}(f_{k}-f_{k-1})+D_{1}+D_{2}+D_{3}

\displaystyle=f(t_{1})g(t_{1})h(t_{1})-f(t_{0})g(t_{0})h(t_{0})-\sum_{k=1}^{K}(f_{k}-f_{k-1})(g_{k}-g_{k-1})h_{k-1}+D_{1}+D_{2}+D_{3}.

Since
t_{k}-t_{k-1}\leq\lVert\mathfrak{p}\rVert<\delta
, we have

\left\lvert\sum_{k=1}^{K}(f_{k}-f_{k-1})(g_{k}-g_{k-1})h_{k-1}\right\rvert\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K}\varepsilon\lvert g_{k}-g_{k-1}\rvert\lVert h\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq\varepsilon{\operatorname{Var}}_{t_{0},t_{1}}\lVert h\rVert_{L^{\infty}},

and therefore

\displaystyle\left\lvert\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}f(t)g(t)\,\mathrm{d}h(t)+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}f(t)h(t)\,\mathrm{d}g(t)+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}g(t)h(t)\,\mathrm{d}f(t)-\big(f(t_{1})g(t_{1})h(t_{1})-f(t_{0})g(t_{0})h(t_{0})\big)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert\sum_{k=1}^{K}(f_{k}-f_{k-1})(g_{k}-g_{k-1})h_{k-1}\right\rvert+\lvert D_{1}\rvert+\lvert D_{2}\rvert+\lvert D_{3}\rvert<\varepsilon({\operatorname{Var}}_{t_{0},t_{1}}\lVert h\rVert_{L^{\infty}}+3).

Since
\varepsilon
can be chosen arbitrarily small, we conclude that (
70
) holds.
∎

D.1
The matrix Riemann-Stieltjes integral

We extend the Riemann-Stieltjes integral to matrix-valued functions in the following way.

Definition D.7
.

Given three matrix functions
F=[f_{ij}]:[t_{0},t_{1}]\to\mathbb{C}^{m,n}
,
G=[g_{jk}]:[t_{0},t_{1}]\to\mathbb{C}^{n,p}
and
Z=[z_{kl}]:[t_{0},t_{1}]\to\mathbb{C}^{p,q}
, the
Riemann-Stieltjes integral
of
F
and
Z
with respect to
G
is defined (when it exists) as

\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\big(F(t)\,\mathrm{d}G(t)Z(t)\big)\coloneqq\left[\sum_{j,k}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}f_{ij}(t)z_{kl}(t)\,\mathrm{d}g_{jk}(t)\right]_{i,l}\in\mathbb{C}^{m,q},

(71)

consistently with matrix multiplication. If the Riemann-Stieltjes integral exists, we write
(F,Z)\in\mathcal{R}_{G}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{m,q})
, or, in short,
(F,Z)\in\mathcal{R}_{G}
, when
[t_{0},t_{1}]
and
\mathbb{C}^{m,q}
are clear from the context.

It is also useful to consider the following equivalent definition of the matrix Riemann-Stieltjes integral.

Lemma D.8
.

Let
F=[f_{ij}]:[t_{0},t_{1}]\to\mathbb{C}^{m,n}
,
G=[g_{jk}]:[t_{0},t_{1}]\to\mathbb{C}^{n,p}
and
Z=[z_{kl}]:[t_{0},t_{1}]\to\mathbb{C}^{p,q}
such that
(F,Z)\in\mathcal{R}_{G}
. Then

\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\big(F(t)\,\mathrm{d}G(t)Z(t)\big)=\lim_{\lVert\mathfrak{p}\rVert\to 0}\sum_{k=1}^{K}F(\widetilde{s}_{k})\big(G(\widetilde{t}_{k})-G(\widetilde{t}_{k-1})\big)Z(\widetilde{s}_{k}),

(72)

where for every partition
\mathfrak{p}=\{\widetilde{t}_{0},\ldots,\widetilde{t}_{K}\}\in\mathfrak{P}_{[t_{0},t_{1}]}
we take any
\widetilde{s}_{k}\in[\widetilde{t}_{k-1},\widetilde{t}_{k}]
, for
k=1,\ldots,K
.

Proof.

From the definition of the scalar Riemann-Stieltjes integral and of matrix multiplication we obtain that

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\big(F(t)\,\mathrm{d}G(t)Z(t)\big)

\displaystyle=\left[\sum_{j,k}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}f_{ij}(t)z_{kl}(t)\,\mathrm{d}g_{jk}(t)\right]_{i,l}

\displaystyle=\left[\sum_{j,k}\lim_{\lVert\mathfrak{p}\rVert\to 0}\sum_{m=1}^{K}f_{ij}(\widetilde{s}_{m})z_{kl}(\widetilde{s}_{m})\big(g_{jk}(\widetilde{t}_{m})-g_{jk}(\widetilde{t}_{m-1})\big)\right]_{i,l}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\lVert\mathfrak{p}\rVert\to 0}\sum_{m=1}^{K}\left[\sum_{j,k}f_{ij}(\widetilde{s}_{m})z_{kl}(\widetilde{s}_{m})\big(g_{jk}(\widetilde{t}_{m})-g_{jk}(\widetilde{t}_{m-1})\big)\right]_{i,l}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\lVert\mathfrak{p}\rVert\to 0}\sum_{m=1}^{K}F(\widetilde{s}_{m})\big(G(\widetilde{t}_{m})-G(\widetilde{t}_{m-1})\big)Z(\widetilde{s}_{m}).\qed

We define implicitly

\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}(F\,\mathrm{d}G)\coloneqq\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\big(F(\,\mathrm{d}G)I_{p}\big),\qquad\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\big((\,\mathrm{d}G)Z\big)\coloneqq\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\big(I_{n}(\,\mathrm{d}G)Z\big),

and

\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\,\mathrm{d}G\coloneqq\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\big(I_{n}(\,\mathrm{d}G)I_{p}\big)=G(t_{1})-G(t_{0}),

which follows immediately from
Lemma

D.8
.
We proceed by extending to the matrix-valued case several statements that are true for the scalar Riemann-Stieltjes integral.

Lemma D.9
.

Let
F\in\mathcal{C}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{m,n})
,
G\in\BV([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{n,p})
and
Z\in\mathcal{C}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{p,q})
. Then
(F,Z)\in\mathcal{R}_{G}
and

\left\lVert\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\big(F(t)\,\mathrm{d}G(t)Z(t)\big)\right\rVert_{2}\leq\lVert F\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert Z\rVert_{L^{\infty}}{\operatorname{Var}}_{t_{0},t_{1}}(G),

(73)

where
{\operatorname{Var}}_{t_{0},t_{1}}(G)
denotes the total variation of
G
.

Proof.

Let
F=[f_{ij}]
,
G=[g_{jk}]
and
Z=[z_{kl}]
denote the entries of the matrix functions.
Since
F
and
Z
are continuous and
G
is of bounded variation, then
f_{ij},z_{kl}\in\mathcal{C}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C})
and
g_{jk}\in\BV([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C})
for all
i,j,k,l
,
and therefore the Riemann-Stieltjes integral
\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}f_{ij}(t)z_{kl}(t)\,\mathrm{d}g_{jk}(t)
exists for all
i,j,k,l
, because of
Theorem

D.4
.
We conclude that the matrix Riemann-Stieltjes integral is well-defined.

To obtain the inequality, we observe with the alternative definition (
72
) that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\sum_{k=1}^{K}F(\widetilde{s}_{k})\big(G(\widetilde{t}_{k})-G(\widetilde{t}_{k-1})\big)Z(\widetilde{s}_{k})\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K}\lVert F\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert Z\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert G(\widetilde{t}_{k})-G(\widetilde{t}_{k-1})\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\lVert F\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert Z\rVert_{L^{\infty}}{\operatorname{Var}}_{t_{0},t_{1}}(G)

holds for every partition
\mathfrak{p}=\{\widetilde{t}_{k}\}\in\mathfrak{P}_{[t_{0},t_{1}]}
and corresponding choice of
\widetilde{s}_{k}
, and therefore

\left\lVert\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\big(F(t)\,\mathrm{d}G(t)Z(t)\big)\right\rVert_{2}\leq\lVert F\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert Z\rVert_{L^{\infty}}{\operatorname{Var}}_{t_{0},t_{1}}(G),

by passing to the limit.
∎

Lemma D.10
.

Let
F\in\mathcal{C}\cap\BV([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{m,n})
,
G\in\BV([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{n,p})
and
Z\in\mathcal{C}\cap\BV([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{p,q})
. Then
(AG,I)\in\mathcal{R}_{B}
,
(A,B)\in\mathcal{R}_{G}
and
(I,GB)\in\mathcal{R}_{A}
.
Furthermore, the integration by parts formula

F(t_{1})G(t_{1})Z(t_{1})-F(t_{0})G(t_{0})Z(t_{0})=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\big(FG(\,\mathrm{d}Z)\big)+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\big(F(\,\mathrm{d}G)Z\big)+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\big((\,\mathrm{d}F)GZ\big)

(74)

holds.

Proof.

By applying
Lemma

D.6
entrywise, we obtain

\displaystyle F(t_{1})G(t_{1})Z(t_{1})-F(t_{0})G(t_{0})Z(t_{0})=\left[\sum_{j,k}\big(f_{ij}(t_{1})g_{jk}(t_{1})z_{kl}(t_{1})-f_{ij}(t_{0})g_{jk}(t_{0})z_{kl}(t_{0})\big)\right]_{i,l}

\displaystyle=\left[\sum_{j,k}\left(\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}f_{ij}g_{jk}\,\mathrm{d}z_{kl}+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}f_{ij}z_{jk}\,\mathrm{d}g_{kl}+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}g_{ij}z_{jk}\,\mathrm{d}f_{kl}\right)\right]_{i,l}

\displaystyle=\left[\sum_{j,k}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}f_{ij}g_{jk}\,\mathrm{d}z_{kl}\right]_{i,l}+\left[\sum_{j,k}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}f_{ij}z_{jk}\,\mathrm{d}g_{kl}\right]_{i,l}+\left[\sum_{j,k}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}g_{ij}z_{jk}\,\mathrm{d}f_{kl}\right]_{i,l}

\displaystyle=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\big(F(t)G(t)\,\mathrm{d}Z(t)\big)+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\big(F(t)\,\mathrm{d}G(t)Z(t)\big)+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\big(\,\mathrm{d}F(t)G(t)Z(t)\big).\qed

Lemma D.11
.

If
F\in\mathcal{C}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{m,n})
,
G\in W^{1,1}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{n,p})
and
Z\in\mathcal{C}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{p,q})
, then

\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\big(F(t)\,\mathrm{d}G(t)Z(t)\big)=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}F(t)\dot{G}(t)Z(t)\,\mathrm{d}t.

Proof.

By applying
Theorem

D.5
entrywise we obtain

\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\big(F(\,\mathrm{d}G)Z\big)=\left[\sum_{j,k}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}f_{ij}z_{kl}\,\mathrm{d}g_{jk}\right]_{i,l}=\left[\sum_{j,k}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}f_{ij}\dot{g}_{jk}z_{kl}\,\mathrm{d}t\right]_{i,l}=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}F\dot{G}Z\,\,\mathrm{d}t.\qed

We next show that the matrix Riemann-Stieltjes integral satisfies something similar to the fundamental theorem of calculus.

Lemma D.12
.

Let
F\in\mathcal{C}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{m,n})
,
G\in\BV([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{n,p})
, and
Z\in\mathcal{C}([t_{0},t_{1}],\mathbb{C}^{p,q})
.
Then for every
\varepsilon>0
there is
\delta>0
such that, for every
h\in[0,\delta]
,
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}-\delta]
and
s\in[t,t+h]
it holds that

\left\lVert\int_{t}^{t+h}\big(F(\,\mathrm{d}G)Z\big)-F(s)\big(G(t+h)-G(t)\big)Z(s)\right\rVert_{2}\leq\varepsilon{\operatorname{Var}}_{t,t+h}(G).

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon_{1}>0
be an arbitrarily small positive constant that we are going to select later.
Since
F
and
Z
are continuous matrix functions on a compact interval, there is
\delta>0
such that
\lVert F(s_{1})-F(s_{0})\rVert<\varepsilon_{1}
and
\lVert Z(s_{1})-Z(s_{0})\rVert<\varepsilon_{1}
for every
s_{0},s_{1}\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
such that
\lvert s_{1}-s_{0}\rvert<\delta
.
For every
h\in[0,\delta]
and
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}-h]
let then
\mathfrak{p}=\{\widetilde{t}_{0},\ldots,\widetilde{t}_{K}\}\in\mathfrak{P}_{[t,t+h]}
be a partition such that

S_{1}\coloneqq\left\lVert\int_{t}^{t+h}\big(F(\,\mathrm{d}G)Z\big)-\sum_{k=1}^{K}F(\widetilde{t}_{k})\big(G(\widetilde{t}_{k})-G(\widetilde{t}_{k-1})\big)Z(\widetilde{t}_{k})\right\rVert\leq\varepsilon_{1}{\operatorname{Var}}_{t,t+h}(G).

For every
s\in[t,t+h]
it holds that

\displaystyle S_{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\lVert\sum_{k=1}^{K}F(\widetilde{t}_{k})\big(G(\widetilde{t}_{k})-G(\widetilde{t}_{k-1})\big)Z(\widetilde{t}_{k})-F(s)\big(G(t+h)-G(t)\big)Z(s)\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\sum_{k=1}^{K}\Big(F(\widetilde{t}_{k})\big(G(\widetilde{t}_{k})-G(\widetilde{t}_{k-1})\big)Z(\widetilde{t}_{k})-F(s)\big(G(\widetilde{t}_{k})-G(\widetilde{t}_{k-1})\big)Z(s)\Big)\right\rVert
